$\phi(P\cup C)-v(P\cup C)\leq\phi(C)-v(C)$, which is precisely the
off-side slack-domination hypothesis.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[exact-face compensation identity]
\label{lem:lx-generator-compensation}
Let $v$ be exact, let $P=N\setminus S$, and let $\psi\in\core(v)$.  For
nonempty $C\subsetneq S$, choose any $a\in\core(v)$ with
$a(P\cup C)=v(P\cup C)$; such an $a$ exists by exactness.  Then
\[
 v(P\cup C)-v(C)-\psi(P)
 =[a(P)-\psi(P)]+[a(C)-v(C)].
 \tag{GC}\label{eq:lx-generator-compensation}
\]
In particular, $a(P)\geq\psi(P)$ is a sufficient one-way screen for
$v(P\cup C)-v(C)\geq\psi(P)$.  When $\psi=\phi$, this is the off-side
hypothesis used in Proposition~\ref{prop:lx-carrier-capacity}; it is not an
equivalent characterization, because the nonnegative trace compensation
$a(C)-v(C)$ may rescue cases with $a(P)<\psi(P)$.  The algebraic identity
itself is not restricted to a finite lower-envelope representation.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $a(P\cup C)=v(P\cup C)$ and $P\cap C=\varnothing$, the identity is
algebraic.  Core feasibility gives $a(C)-v(C)\geq0$.
\end{proof}

The exact interval does not rescue proper-coalition monotonicity.  In fact the
failure already occurs with five players, zero singleton floors, and a
two-generator endpoint.

\begin{theorem}[proper-coalition monotonicity fails on exact games]
\label{thm:lx-proper-exact-fails}
Let $N=\{1,2,3,4,5\}$, $S=\{1,3,4,5\}$, and let $v$ and $w$ be the
lower-envelope games generated respectively by
\[
 A=(2,0,12,6,0),\qquad B=(0,0,0,0,20),\qquad
 P=(0,2,10,4,4)
\]
and by $A,B$ alone.  Then both games are exact, all singleton values are zero,
and they differ only at $S$, where
\[
 v(S)=18<20=w(S).
\]
Nevertheless
\[
 \phi(v)=\left(1,1,\frac{13}{2},5,\frac{13}{2}\right),
 \qquad
 \phi(w)=(2,0,6,6,6).
 \tag{PM}\label{eq:lx-proper-exact-counterexample}
\]
Thus target players $3$ and $5$ each lose $1/2$.
Because both games have zero singleton values, this is also a direct failure
of proper-coalition monotonicity for the ordinary Lmin solution.

More precisely, if $v^\tau$ agrees with $v$ off $S$ and
$v^\tau(S)=\tau$, then every $v^\tau$, $18\leq\tau\leq20$, is exact and
\[
 \phi(v^\tau)=
 \begin{cases}
  \phi(v),&18\leq\tau\leq19,\\[2mm]
  (\tau-18,\ 20-\tau,\ 16-\tau/2,\ \tau-14,\ 16-\tau/2),
       &19\leq\tau\leq20.
 \end{cases}
 \tag{PP}\label{eq:lx-proper-exact-path}
\]
The losses therefore begin immediately when the bump becomes binding.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The three displayed allocations have common total $20$.  The additional
generator $P$ lowers only $S$.  Indeed, if $5\notin T$, then
$B(T)=0\leq P(T)$.  If $5\in T$, then
\[
 P(T)-A(T)=4+2\mathbf1_{\{2\in T\}}
       -2|T\cap\{1,3,4\}|,
\]
which is negative only when $T=S$.  At that coalition $P(S)=18$ while
$A(S)=B(S)=20$.  Thus $v$ and $w$ agree off $S$.  Every generator belongs
to the corresponding core and attains the coalitions on which it is minimal,
so both lower-envelope games are exact.  Directly, their common singleton
floors are zero.

Here is a hand certificate for the first point in
\eqref{eq:lx-proper-exact-counterexample}.  In the base game
$v(345)=18$, so efficiency gives $x_1+x_2\leq2$.  Hence the first common
gain is at most one, and equality pins players $1,2$.  On that face the
remaining total is $18$, while $v(135)=14$ gives
$x_3+x_5\geq13$ and therefore $x_4\leq5$.  This pins player~4; efficiency
leaves $x_3+x_5=13$, which the last stage equalizes.  The resulting point is
the displayed $\phi(v)$ and direct substitution places it in the core.

For the endpoint, $w(S)=20$ and efficiency pin player~2 at zero.  Next
$w(345)=18$ gives $x_1\leq2$.  After player~1 is pinned at two, the remaining
total is $18$ and $w(35)=12$ gives $x_4\leq6$; the last pair is equalized.
This gives $\phi(w)=(2,0,6,6,6)$, again core-feasible by direct substitution.

For the path, $\phi(v)(S)=19$, so the nonbinding part is constant.  Suppose
$19\leq\tau\leq20$.  The target constraint and efficiency first give
$x_2\leq20-\tau$.  Once player~2 is pinned, $v(345)=18$ gives
$x_1\leq\tau-18$.  On the resulting face $x(345)=18$; using
$v(135)=14$ gives $x_4\leq\tau-14$.  Finally
$x_3+x_5=32-\tau$, so the last stage equalizes them.  These four bounds give
\eqref{eq:lx-proper-exact-path}.  Every coordinate is affine, and direct
core feasibility at $\tau=19,20$ proves feasibility throughout the segment.
Exactness throughout is Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-interval}.

The exact rational verifier recomputes both endpoint Lmin allocations by a
staged Maschler LP, rather than merely checking the displayed stage bounds:
\begin{center}
\small\path{src/verify_n5_exact_leximin_monotonicity_counterexample.py},\\[-2pt]
\small\path{results/n5_exact_leximin_monotonicity_counterexample.json}.
\end{center}
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[a large core without a Lorenz-dominating element]
\label{prop:lx-five-large-core-separation}
Both five-player games $v,w$ in
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-proper-exact-fails} have large cores, and
\[
 \operatorname{Lmax}(v)=\operatorname{Lmax}(w)=(2,0,6,6,6).
\]
Thus Lmax is coalitionally monotonic across this bump while Lmin is not.  In
the base game the richest Lmin stratum is $\{3,5\}$ and
\[
 \operatorname{Lmin}(v)(\{3,5\})=13>12=v(\{3,5\}).
\]
The base game is not an exact partition game at any core allocation and its
core has no Lorenz-dominating element.  The endpoint game is an exact partition
game, witnessed by its common Lmin--Lmax allocation with ordered strata
$\{2\}\prec\{1\}\prec\{3,4,5\}$.  Consequently largeness alone implies
neither the exact-partition property nor existence of a Lorenz-dominating core
element, and the through-four-player classification below is sharp at five.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Computer-assisted proof]
For each game, enumerate every vertex of Sharkey's acceptable-payoff
polyhedron
\[
 U(v)=\{y\in\mathbb R^N:y(T)\geq v(T)\text{ for every nonempty }T\subseteq N\}.
\]
There are $\binom{31}{5}=169{,}911$ candidate tight bases, of which $104{,}286$
are independent.  Exact rational elimination gives $12$ distinct acceptable
vertices for $v$ and $8$ for $w$.  An exact core LP finds, for every such
vertex $y$, an $x\in\core(v)$ or $x\in\core(w)$ with $x\leq y$.
Since $U(v)$ is pointed with nonnegative recession cone, convexity extends
vertex domination to all of $U(v)$; this is the large-core property.  Separate
exact staged LPs compute the two Lmax allocations, and direct substitution
checks the displayed strict suffix slack.

To test the exact-partition property independently of Lmin, enumerate all
$541$ ordered set partitions $B_1\prec\cdots\prec B_k$ of five players.  For
each chain, an exact rational linear-arithmetic query imposes core feasibility,
equal payoffs within each block, strict payoff inequalities between adjacent
blocks, and tightness of every richer suffix
$B_{j+1}\cup\cdots\cup B_k$.  All $541$ base-game queries are infeasible.  For
the endpoint, the displayed three-block chain is feasible exactly at
$(2,0,6,6,6)$.  Finally, any core allocation that Lorenz-dominated the whole
core would be both its leximin and leximax point.  The displayed inequality
$\operatorname{Lmin}(v)\ne\operatorname{Lmax}(v)$ therefore excludes such an
allocation in the base game.  The verifier and output are
\begin{center}
\small\path{src/verify_n5_exact_lmin_lmax_large_core.py},\\[-2pt]
\small\path{results/n5_exact_lmin_lmax_large_core.json}.
\end{center}
\end{proof}

The example also closes the proposed cone route.  For every
$19\leq\tau<20$, the core point
\[
 y^\tau=(2,0,16-\tau/2,\tau-14,16-\tau/2)
\]
weakly dominates $\phi(v^\tau)$ on $S$ and has $y^\tau(S)=20>\tau$.
Thus $(\star_S)$ holds along the entire binding interior, even while players
$3$ and $5$ decrease.  The implication ``$(\star_S)$ along an exact path
implies coordinatewise monotonicity'' is therefore false; the carrier
program controls exact bump capacity but cannot prove monotonicity of this
selector.

This negative result should be read against the classical impossibility
literature, not as an unrestricted first impossibility theorem.
\citet{ArinInarra2001} introduced Lmin, and \citet{Dietzenbacher2021}
already shows that Lmin can fail aggregate monotonicity on the broader
egalitarian-stable domain while Lmax is coalitionally monotonic there.
Moreover, \citet{HousmanClark1998} prove that no core allocation method can
be coalitionally monotonic on the full balanced-game domain once there are at
least four players.  None of these results itself supplies an exact-game
proper-coalition counterexample for Lmin.  Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-proper-exact-fails}
does so with a five-player rational certificate.  The next result shows that
five is the minimum possible dimension.
The condition needed next is established terminology.  Following
\citet{LlerenaMauri2017}, a balanced game is an \emph{exact partition game} if
it has a core allocation at which every cumulative union of richest payoff
strata is tight.  On this class the Dutta--Ray solution is the unique such
allocation and Lorenz-dominates the core
\citep{LlerenaMauri2017,DietzenbacherYanovskaya2020}.  The new contribution is
the following sharp low-dimensional classification, not the definition or its
Lorenz consequence.

We will use one further established description of exactness.  An
\emph{exact-balanced} weight vector $\rho$ satisfies
$\sum_S\rho_S\mathbf1_S=\mathbf1_N$, with every nonzero coefficient positive
except possibly one.  A game is exact if and only if
$\sum_S\rho_Sv(S)\leq v(N)$ for every such vector
\citep{CsokaHeringsKoczy2011,LohmannBormHerings2012}.  At a core allocation
$\phi$, write $s_\phi(S)=\phi(S)-v(S)$.

\begin{lemma}[exact-balanced slack budget]
\label{lem:lx-exact-balanced-slack}
If $v$ is exact, $\phi\in\core(v)$, and $\rho$ is exact-balanced, then
\[
 \sum_S\rho_Ss_\phi(S)\geq0.                          \tag{SB}
 \label{eq:lx-slack-budget}
\]
If $H$ is the unique negatively weighted coalition, then
\[
 (-\rho_H)s_\phi(H)\leq
 \sum_{S\ne H}\rho_Ss_\phi(S).                       \tag{SB-H}
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The incidence identity and efficiency give
$\sum_S\rho_S\phi(S)=\phi(N)=v(N)$.  Subtract the exact-balancedness
inequality $\sum_S\rho_Sv(S)\leq v(N)$.
\end{proof}

The next quantitative form exposes how tight transfer-blockers can be
completed by singleton coalitions into an exact-balanced collection.

\begin{lemma}[boundary-cover inequality]
\label{lem:lx-boundary-cover}
Let $v$ be a singleton-normalized exact game, let $\phi\in\core(v)$, and fix
$\emptyset\ne H\subsetneq N$, with $L=N\setminus H$ and
$s=\phi(H)-v(H)$.  Give coalitions tight at $\phi$ arbitrary weights
$\lambda_T\geq0$, and put
\[
 c_j=\sum_{T\ni j}\lambda_T.
\]
If $\alpha>\beta\geq0$, $c_h\leq\alpha$ for $h\in H$, and
$c_i\leq\beta$ for $i\in L$, then
\[
 (\alpha-\beta)s\leq
 \sum_{h\in H}(\alpha-c_h)\phi_h
 +\sum_{i\in L}(\beta-c_i)\phi_i.                    \tag{BC}
 \label{eq:lx-boundary-cover}
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Exactness of $H$ provides $z\in\core(v)$ with $z(H)=v(H)$.  For
$d=z-\phi$,
\[
 d(H)=-s,\qquad d(L)=s,\qquad d_j\geq-\phi_j,
 \qquad d(T)\geq0
\]
for every coalition $T$ tight at $\phi$.  Therefore
\[
 0\leq\sum_T\lambda_Td(T)=\sum_jc_jd_j
 \leq-(\alpha-\beta)s
 +\sum_{h\in H}(\alpha-c_h)\phi_h
 +\sum_{i\in L}(\beta-c_i)\phi_i,
\]
which is \eqref{eq:lx-boundary-cover}.
\end{proof}

For its exact-balanced interpretation, add singleton weight
$\alpha-c_h$ at each $h\in H$ and $\beta-c_i$ at each $i\in L$, and give
$H$ weight $-(\alpha-\beta)$.  The resulting coverage is
$\beta\mathbf1_N$; after adding a common grand-coalition weight when
$\beta=0$ and rescaling, Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-exact-balanced-slack} gives the
same inequality.  Thus \eqref{eq:lx-boundary-cover} is a quantitative
protector-completion form of published exact balancedness, not a new
characterization of exact games.

Singleton completion can be sharpened to an intrinsic coalition-slack cost.
This form needs no normalization once the tight cover is given.

\begin{theorem}[tight deficit completion]
\label{thm:lx-tight-deficit-completion}
Let $v$ be exact, $\phi\in\core(v)$, and
$N=H\mathbin{\dot\cup}L$. Suppose weights $\lambda_T\geq0$ on
$\phi$-tight coalitions have common load $\alpha$ on $H$ and loads
$c_i\leq\beta<\alpha$ on $i\in L$. Put $d_i=\beta-c_i$. If weights
$\nu_Q\geq0$ on nonempty coalitions $Q\subseteq L$ satisfy
\[
 \sum_{Q\subseteq L}\nu_Q\mathbf1_Q=d,
\]
then
\[
 (\alpha-\beta)s_\phi(H)\leq
 \sum_{Q\subseteq L}\nu_Qs_\phi(Q).                 \tag{DC}
 \label{eq:lx-tight-deficit-completion}
\]
Consequently, if $d$ lies in the incidence cone of $\phi$-tight coalitions
contained in $L$, then $H$ is tight.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The cover and completion give
\[
 \sum_T\lambda_T\mathbf1_T+\sum_Q\nu_Q\mathbf1_Q
 -(\alpha-\beta)\mathbf1_H=\beta\mathbf1_N.
\]
After rescaling when $\beta>0$, this is exact-balanced. If $\beta=0$, first
add any positive common grand-coalition coefficient; its slack is zero.
Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-exact-balanced-slack}, tightness of every $T$, and
$s_\phi(N)=0$ now give \eqref{eq:lx-tight-deficit-completion}.
\end{proof}

The displayed incidence system is a semi-balanced system with exceptional
set $H$ in the terminology of \citet{StudenyKratochvil2022}; its associated
exact-game inequality is prior art. The candidate new object below is the
restricted optimization selected by an Lmin tight cover, not the
semi-balanced inequality itself.

Minimizing the right side over all completions defines a canonical LP value
$\kappa_{\phi,L}(d)$. Singleton coalitions make the LP feasible, while
nonnegative slack makes its optimum nonnegative and attained. Thus
\[
 (\alpha-\beta)s_\phi(H)\leq\kappa_{\phi,L}(d),
 \qquad
 \kappa_{\phi,L}(d)=0
 \Longleftrightarrow
 d\in\operatorname{cone}\{\mathbf1_Q:Q\subseteq L,\ s_\phi(Q)=0\}.
 \tag{DC-K}\label{eq:lx-completion-cost}
\]
This turns every slack boundary into a positive-cost ray of a poorer-side
tight-set cone. The five-player two-column family and the seven-player
three-trace family both attain equality: their respective parameters are
$(\alpha,\beta)=(2,1)$ with completion $\mathbf1_R$, and
$(1,1/2)$ with completion $\tfrac12\mathbf1_{\{a\}}$. A standalone proof and
exact audit are
\begin{center}
\small\path{paper3/TIGHT_DEFICIT_COMPLETION_THEOREM.md},\\[-2pt]
\small\path{src/verify_tight_deficit_completion.py},\\[-2pt]
\small\path{results/tight_deficit_completion.json}.
\end{center}

Equivalently, define the fractional exact-cover worth
\[
 \omega_{v,L}(d):=\max\left\{
   \sum_Q\nu_Qv(Q):\nu_Q\geq0,
   \ \sum_Q\nu_Q\mathbf1_Q=d,\ \emptyset\ne Q\subseteq L\right\}.
\]
Modularity of $\phi$ gives the exact gap identity
\[
 \kappa_{\phi,L}(d)=d\mathbin{\cdot}\phi_L-\omega_{v,L}(d). \tag{DC-G}
 \label{eq:lx-completion-gap}
\]
Thus coalitional completion is never more expensive than singleton
completion in a normalized game, and it is strictly cheaper whenever an
optimal completion uses positive total coalition worth. The invariant is a
fractional exact-cover value gap, not merely a renamed uncovered-payoff sum.

The same inequality has a useful internal-face specialization.  It is the
common budget law behind both the unique-richest threshold and the later
trace-rounding exclusions.

\begin{corollary}[exact-face overload budget]
\label{cor:lx-exact-face-overload}
Let $v$ be a singleton-normalized exact game, let $\phi\in\core(v)$, and let
$\varnothing\ne D\subseteq H\subsetneq N$.  Suppose nonnegative real weights
on proper $\phi$-tight coalitions have incidence loads
\[
 c_h=1+q\mathbf1_{\{h\in D\}}\quad(h\in H),
 \qquad 0\leq c_i\leq1\quad(i\in L:=N\setminus H)       \tag{OF}
 \label{eq:lx-exact-face-overload}
\]
for some $q>0$.  Then
\[
 q\,s_\phi(D)
 \leq\sum_{i\in L}(1-c_i)\phi_i.                       \tag{OB}
 \label{eq:lx-exact-face-overload-budget}
\]
In particular, if the cover loads every player outside $H$ once, then $D$
is tight at $\phi$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-boundary-cover} with target coalition $D$,
$\alpha=1+q$, and $\beta=1$.  The players in $H\setminus D$ have load one,
the players in $L$ have load at most one, and the completion terms on $D$
and $H\setminus D$ vanish.  The resulting inequality is
\eqref{eq:lx-exact-face-overload-budget}.  If every poorer load is one, its
right side is zero; core feasibility gives $s_\phi(D)\geq0$.
\end{proof}

Equivalently, exactify $D$ at a core point $x$.  Summing the tight-coalition
inequalities gives $x(H)+qv(D)+\sum_{i\in L}c_ix_i$ on the left.  Singleton
normalization makes $x_i\geq0$, so replacing the poorer loads by one can
only increase that side; efficiency then gives
\eqref{eq:lx-exact-face-overload-budget}.  This direct proof emphasizes that
the overloaded set may be any rich coalition, not merely one player, and
that the cover weights need not be integral.

For a fixed family $\mathcal T$ of $\phi$-tight coalitions, normalize
$\alpha-\beta=1$ in \eqref{eq:lx-boundary-cover} and minimize its right side
over $\lambda_T\geq0$ and $\beta\geq0$, subject to
$c_h\leq\beta+1$ on $H$ and $c_i\leq\beta$ on $L$.  Denote this finite
linear-program value by $\Delta_{\phi,\mathcal T}(H)$; taking all weights zero
shows $\Delta_{\phi,\mathcal T}(H)\leq\phi(H)$.

\begin{corollary}[boundary-defect bound]
\label{cor:lx-boundary-defect}
Under the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-boundary-cover},
\[
 0\leq s_\phi(H)\leq\Delta_{\phi,\mathcal T}(H)\leq\phi(H).
 \tag{BD}\label{eq:lx-boundary-defect}
\]
In particular, a zero-defect tight-coalition cover forces $H$ to be tight.
If $\phi=\operatorname{Lmin}(v)$ and such a cover exists at every strict
payoff boundary, then $v$ is an exact partition game witnessed at $\phi$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The middle inequality is Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-boundary-cover} for every feasible
cover, followed by minimization.  Core feasibility gives the first inequality,
and the zero-weight cover gives the last.  The final assertion applies the
zero-defect conclusion to every cumulative richer suffix.
\end{proof}

The following transfer-blocking property is not new: it is the
egalitarian-core characterization of \citet{ArinInarra2001}, applied to Lmin.
We use ``protector coalition'' only as a descriptive label.  The new use below
is to combine this published property with exact-game singleton witnesses,
replacing the finite stratum enumeration used in an earlier version.

\begin{lemma}[protector coalition]\label{lem:lx-protector}
Let $\phi=\operatorname{Lmin}(v)$, and order its payoff strata
$B_1,\ldots,B_k$ from lowest to highest.  If $i\in B_j$ and
$h\in B_{j+1}\cup\cdots\cup B_k$, then some coalition $T$ is tight at
$\phi$ and satisfies
\[
 h\in T,\qquad i\notin T.
 \tag{PC}\label{eq:lx-protector}
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The claim follows directly from the transfer-blocking characterization of the
egalitarian core in \citet{ArinInarra2001}, since Lmin belongs to that set.
For completeness, the following LP-dual derivation also checks the paper's
inequality conventions.  The face is indexed by final payoff strata rather
than by possibly finer algorithmic pinning substeps; tied players that pin at
different degenerate substeps therefore cause no ambiguity.
Put $\alpha=\phi_i$ and $P=B_1\cup\cdots\cup B_{j-1}$.  On the stratum face
\[
 F=\{x\in\core(v):x_p=\phi_p\ (p\in P),\quad
                    x_\ell\geq\alpha\ (\ell\notin P)\},
\]
the maximum of $x_i$ is $\alpha$.  Indeed, if $x_i>\alpha$ at some
$x\in F$, then a sufficiently short segment from $\phi$ toward $x$ keeps
every richer coordinate strictly above $\alpha$, keeps all coordinates in
$B_j$ at least $\alpha$, and raises coordinate $i$.  This lexicographically
improves $\phi$.

Apply LP duality to $\max_{x\in F}x_i$.  Let $\lambda_S\geq0$ multiply
$v(S)-x(S)\leq0$, let $\mu_\ell\geq0$ multiply
$\alpha-x_\ell\leq0$, and write
$w_q=\sum_{S\ni q}\lambda_S$.  Stationarity at a coordinate outside $P$ is
\[
 -\mathbf1_{\{q=i\}}-w_q-\mu_q+\nu=0.
\]
Coordinates in $P$ instead carry free equality multipliers and impose no
additional restriction on this coverage comparison.
The floor of the richer player $h$ is strictly inactive, so $\mu_h=0$.
Consequently
\[
 w_h-w_i=1+\mu_i>0.
\]
Some positive-multiplier coalition contains $h$ and omits $i$; complementary
slackness makes it tight at $\phi$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[fractional protector-packing obstruction]
\label{lem:lx-protector-packing}
Let $v$ be a normalized exact game and let
$\phi=\operatorname{Lmin}(v)$ have a unique richest player $r$, with
$\phi_r=\gamma$.  Put $L=N\setminus\{r\}$ and $q_i=\phi_i$ for $i\in L$.
For each $i\in L$, Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-protector} supplies a tight protector
$\{r\}\cup Q_i$ omitting $i$.  It has
$\emptyset\ne Q_i\subseteq L\setminus\{i\}$ because
$\gamma>0=v(\{r\})$, so the singleton $\{r\}$ is slack.  Fix any such
choices.  If $\lambda_i\geq0$ and
\[
 c_j:=\sum_{i:j\in Q_i}\lambda_i\leq1\quad(j\in L),
 \qquad \Lambda:=\sum_{i\in L}\lambda_i,
\]
then
\[
 \gamma(\Lambda-1)\leq
 \sum_{j\in L}(1-c_j)q_j.                              \tag{FP}
 \label{eq:lx-fractional-packing}
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $\Lambda\leq1$, the left side of
\eqref{eq:lx-fractional-packing} is nonpositive and the right side is
nonnegative.  If $\Lambda>1$, apply
Corollary~\ref{cor:lx-exact-face-overload} with
$H=D=\{r\}$, $q=\Lambda-1$, and the weighted tight protectors
$T_i=\{r\}\cup Q_i$.  Their poorer loads are the $c_j$, while
$s_\phi(\{r\})=\phi_r-v(\{r\})=\gamma$.  The overload budget is exactly
\eqref{eq:lx-fractional-packing}.
\end{proof}

The packing inequality isolates a sharp sacrifice-budget threshold that is
already visible before the full exact-partition classification.

\begin{theorem}[indecomposable separation family]
\label{thm:lx-unique-richest-threshold}
In a singleton-normalized exact game with $2\leq|N|\leq4$, Lmin cannot have a
unique richest player.  More generally, fix integers $h\geq1$ and
$m\geq h+3$.  There is a four-generator integral exact game on a poorer block
$P$ of size $m$ and a richer block $R$ of size $h$ with
\[
 \operatorname{Lmin}(v)_i=
 \begin{cases}
 h,&i\in P,\\
 m-2,&i\in R,
 \end{cases}
 \qquad v(R)=0.
\]
For the same game, putting
\[
 a=\frac{h^2(m-1)}{(h+1)(m-2)},\qquad
 c=\frac{h(m-1)}{h+1},
\]
one has
\[
 \operatorname{Lmax}(v)_i=
 \begin{cases}
 c,&i\in R\cup\{3,4\},\\
 a,&i\in P\setminus\{3,4\}.
 \end{cases}
\]
Their $\ell^1$ distance is
\[
 \lVert\operatorname{Lmin}(v)-\operatorname{Lmax}(v)\rVert_1
 =\frac{4h(m-h-2)}{h+1}.
\]
Every game in the family is indecomposable, meaning that it admits no
nontrivial direct-sum partition.  Normalizing the distance by the
common total $v(N)=2h(m-1)$ gives
\[
 \frac{\lVert\operatorname{Lmin}(v)-\operatorname{Lmax}(v)\rVert_1}{v(N)}
 =\frac{2(m-h-2)}{(h+1)(m-1)}.
\]
In particular, at $h=1$ the normalized distance is
$(m-3)/(m-1)\to1$.  Thus five is the sharp unique-richest threshold, and an
indecomposable four-generator family has growing Lmin--Lmax separation rather
than merely preserving a fixed example under null-player padding.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Suppose $r$ is uniquely richest, put $L=N\setminus\{r\}$,
$\gamma=\phi_r$, and $q_i=\phi_i<\gamma$.  Choose the protectors in
Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-protector-packing}.  If $|L|=1$, no nonempty $Q_i$ exists.
If $|L|=2$, both $Q_i$ are the opposite singleton; weight both by one in
\eqref{eq:lx-fractional-packing}, giving $\gamma\leq0$.

Let $|L|=3$.  If some $Q_i=\{j\}$, weight the protectors indexed by $i$ and
$j$ by one.  The sets $Q_i$ and $Q_j$ are nonempty and disjoint, so their
union leaves at most one player $u$ uncovered.  Inequality
\eqref{eq:lx-fractional-packing} gives either $\gamma\leq0$ or
$\gamma\leq q_u<\gamma$.  If no $Q_i$ is a singleton, then
$Q_i=L\setminus\{i\}$ for all $i$; weight all three by $1/2$.  Every poorer
load is one and $\Lambda=3/2$, forcing $\gamma/2\leq0$.  This proves the
four-player bound.

For the family, let $P=\{1,\ldots,m\}$ and let $R$ be a disjoint set of
$h$ players.  Write $e_i$ for unit vectors and
$\mathbf1_R=\sum_{r\in R}e_r$.  Take the lower envelope of
\[
\begin{aligned}
g^1&=h(m-1)e_4+(m-1)\mathbf1_R,\\
g^2&=h(m-1)(e_3+e_4),\\
g^3&=h(e_1+e_2+e_3)+h(m-4)e_5+(m-1)\mathbf1_R,\\
g^4&=h\mathbf1_P+(m-2)\mathbf1_R,
\qquad
v(S):=\min_{1\leq k\leq4}g^k(S).
\end{aligned}                                        \tag{UF}
\label{eq:lx-unique-richest-family}
\]
At the endpoint $m=4$, the zero term $h(m-4)e_5$ is simply omitted; player
$5$ then belongs to $R$.
Every row has total $2h(m-1)$ and is a core allocation of the lower-envelope game;
every coalition attains its worth at a row.  Hence $v$ is exact directly from
the definition.  Put $\phi=g^4$.  The four rows give
\[
 v(N\setminus\{1\})=v(N\setminus\{2\})=2h(m-1)-h,
 \qquad v(R\cup\{3\})=v(R\cup\{4\})=h(m-1).   \tag{UF-T}
 \label{eq:lx-unique-richest-family-tight}
\]
The point $\phi$ gives every player at least $h$.  At any core point with all
coordinates at least $h$, the first two displayed constraints and efficiency
force $x_1=x_2=h$.  Write $y=x-h\mathbf1_N$.  Then
$y\geq0$, $y_1=y_2=0$, and $y(N)=h(m-h-2)$.  The last two constraints give
\[
 y_3+y(R)\geq h(m-h-2),\qquad
 y_4+y(R)\geq h(m-h-2).
\]
Comparing each with the total forces $y_i=0$ for every $i\in P$.  Thus the
poorer block is pinned at the first Lmin level $h$.  The richer block has
fixed total $h(m-2)$, so its next common level is at most $m-2$, attained by
$\phi$.  This proves the Lmin formula.  Since $g^2(R)=0$, the richest suffix
$R$ has slack $h(m-2)$.

It remains to identify Lmax.  Every core point satisfies
$x_3+x(R)\geq h(m-1)$ and $x_4+x(R)\geq h(m-1)$ by
\eqref{eq:lx-unique-richest-family-tight}.  Hence its largest coordinate is
at least $c=h(m-1)/(h+1)$.  Put
\[
 t=\frac{h(m-1)}{(h+1)(m-2)}.
\]
The hypothesis $m\geq h+3$ gives $0<t<1$.  The core point
\[
 z=t g^4+(1-t)g^2
\]
has coordinates $z_i=c$ on $R\cup\{3,4\}$ and $z_i=a$ otherwise.  Thus $c$ is the
minimum possible largest coordinate.  On the face where every coordinate is
at most $c$, the two displayed inequalities force every coordinate in
$R\cup\{3,4\}$ to equal $c$.  The remaining $m-2$ coordinates have fixed
total and average $a$; the displayed point attains that common ceiling.  This
proves the Lmax formula and its uniqueness.  Direct subtraction gives the
displayed $\ell^1$ distance.

It remains to prove indecomposability.  In a singleton-normalized direct sum,
two players in different summands have pair worth zero.  Directly from
\eqref{eq:lx-unique-richest-family},
\[
\begin{gathered}
v(\{1,4\})=v(\{2,4\})=v(\{3,4\})=h>0,\\
v(\{3,r\})=v(\{4,r\})=m-1>0\quad(r\in R),
\end{gathered}
\]
and, when $m\geq5$, $v(\{4,5\})>0$.  Thus players $1,\ldots,5$ and all of
$R$ lie in one summand (with the evident $m=4$ interpretation).  If $m=6$,
put $T=\{3,4,5\}\cup R$; then $v(T)=7h$ but
$v(T\cup\{6\})=8h$.  If $m\geq7$, put $T=\{4,5\}$; for every
$j\in P\setminus\{1,\ldots,5\}$,
\[
 v(T)=2h,\qquad v(T\cup\{j\})=3h.
\]
Since every such $T$ is already contained in the first summand and
$v(\{j\})=0$, these strict interactions rule out placing any remaining
poorer player in another summand.  Hence the game is indecomposable.

The escape saturates the exact-balanced circuit
\[
 \mathbf1_{R\cup\{3\}}+\mathbf1_{R\cup\{4\}}
 +\sum_{i\in P\setminus\{3,4\}}\mathbf1_{\{i\}}
 -\mathbf1_R=\mathbf1_N.                            \tag{UF-C}
 \label{eq:lx-unique-richest-family-circuit}
\]
The two tight protector slacks are zero, the $m-2$ uncovered poorer singleton
slacks are $h$ each, and the negative richest-suffix slack is $h(m-2)$.
When $h=1$, this is equivalently equality in
\eqref{eq:lx-fractional-packing}.  In that case, only three poorer players
leave at most one uncovered payoff,
strictly less than the unique richest payoff; from five players onward, the
family supplies exactly the sacrifice budget needed for equality.  The
two-parameter audit, unique-richest verifier, and fixed five-player replay are
recorded in the following paired scripts and artifacts:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item \path{src/verify_rich_block_family.py} and
      \path{results/rich_block_family.json};
\item \path{src/verify_unique_richest_family.py} and
      \path{results/unique_richest_family.json};
\end{itemize}
The fixed five-player replay is indexed in the accompanying structural note.
\end{proof}

The qualifier \emph{indecomposable} is essential to the two-parameter
statement.  A direct sum of $h$ copies of the five-player $h=1$ game trivially
produces $h$ tied rich players and keeps normalized distance $1/3$;
null-player padding likewise preserves dimensional existence.  Neither
operation yields the $h=1$ family's growing normalized distance
$(m-3)/(m-1)\to1$.  For fixed $h$, the displayed family instead tends to
$2/(h+1)$; no uniform claim is made as $h\to\infty$.

The closest published low-dimensional antecedent is that exact games with at
most four players are PES stable \citep{Dietzenbacher2021}; see also the
large-core route in \citet{Biswas1999}.  That solution-stability statement
does not imply exact partition at Lmin, because PES stability is strictly
weaker than the exact-partition tightness condition in general.  The next
lemma supplies the stronger coalitional statement and its sharp structural
explanation.

\begin{lemma}[exact-partition classification through four players]
\label{lem:lx-richer-suffix}
Let $v$ be an exact game with $|N|\leq4$ and $v(\{i\})=0$ for every player.
Order the payoff strata of $\operatorname{Lmin}(v)$ as
$B_1,\ldots,B_k$ from lowest to highest.  At every strict gap, the richer
suffix is tight:
\[
 H_j:=B_{j+1}\cup\cdots\cup B_k,
 \qquad
 \operatorname{Lmin}(v)(H_j)=v(H_j)
 \quad (j<k).
 \tag{RST}\label{eq:lx-richer-suffix}
\]
Equivalently, $v$ is an exact partition game witnessed at its Lmin allocation.
Consequently $\operatorname{Lmin}(v)$ is the Lorenz-dominating core allocation
and equals $\operatorname{Lmax}(v)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Put $\phi=\operatorname{Lmin}(v)$ and consider a strict payoff boundary with
richer suffix $H$ and poorer side $L=N\setminus H$.  If $|H|=1$, its member
is the unique richest player, impossible through four players by
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-unique-richest-threshold}.  Hence $|H|\geq2$, so
$|L|\leq2$.  The human two-poorer boundary theorem proved below,
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-small-poorer-threshold}, now makes $H$ tight (and also
covers $|L|=1$).  This proves \eqref{eq:lx-richer-suffix}; the one-stratum
case is immediate.  Thus the dimension-free balanced-trace transport
obstruction, rather than a four-player payoff-stratum case split, explains
the exact-partition threshold.

For completeness, here is the connection with the published exact-partition
result in the present ordering convention.  Take any $x\in\core(v)$ and let
$H$ consist of the $r$ richest coordinates of $\phi$, at a stratum boundary.
Then
\[
 \sum_{i=1}^{r}x_i^{\downarrow}\geq x(H)\geq v(H)=\phi(H)
   =\sum_{i=1}^{r}\phi_i^{\downarrow}.
\]
Write $U_x(r)=\sum_{i=1}^r x_i^\downarrow$.  Between consecutive boundaries,
$U_x$ is concave in $r$, whereas $U_\phi$ is affine because the intervening
coordinates of $\phi$ are tied.  Endpoint dominance therefore gives
$U_x(r)\geq U_\phi(r)$ throughout the block.  Thus $\phi$ Lorenz-dominates the
core, as in \citet{LlerenaMauri2017}.  If $y$ denotes Lmax, Lorenz dominance
and leximax minimality force $y^\downarrow=\phi^\downarrow$; uniqueness of the
leximax-minimal point of the compact convex core then gives $y=\phi$.
\end{proof}

The five-player certificate also identifies where this mechanism stops.  Its
failed boundary has three poorer and two richer players, with richer pair
$\{3,5\}$ slack.  The only tight nontrivial proper coalitions at the base Lmin
point are $\{3,4,5\}$ and $\{1,3,5\}$: they protect the three poorer players
while retaining both richer players.  More precisely, their incidence vectors
satisfy the exact-balanced identity
\[
 \mathbf1_{\{1,3,5\}}+\mathbf1_{\{3,4,5\}}
 +\mathbf1_{\{2\}}-\mathbf1_{\{3,5\}}=\mathbf1_N.    \tag{E5}
 \label{eq:lx-five-slack-budget}
\]
At the base Lmin allocation the two protector slacks are zero, the singleton
$\{2\}$ has slack one, and the richer suffix $\{3,5\}$ has slack one.
Thus \eqref{eq:lx-slack-budget} is attained at equality: the fifth player
supplies exactly the unit of positive singleton slack that funds the unit of
richer-suffix slack.  Equivalently, \eqref{eq:lx-boundary-cover} is tight with
$\alpha=2$ and $\beta=1$; the sole poorer-side coverage deficit is player~2.
This exact calculation is reproduced by
\path{src/verify_exact_balanced_slack_budget.py}, with output in
\path{results/exact_balanced_slack_budget.json}.  The verifier also solves the
boundary-defect program in exact rational arithmetic.  The displayed cover
has defect one, while \eqref{eq:lx-boundary-defect} gives the matching lower
bound from the unit suffix slack, so the program is sharp in this example.

In particular,
Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-protector-packing} is a unique-richest obstruction and does
not apply; exactness of a singleton can zero one rich player, but not the whole
rich side.  This explains the sharp example's escape without claiming that
every higher-dimensional obstruction has this form.

The number of players below a failed boundary has its own sharp threshold.
The first step is a local dual consequence of the progressive Lmin stages.

The qualitative tight-coalition optimality criterion behind this step is
prior art.  \citet[Lemma~1]{ArinKuipersVermeulen2008} characterize Lmax by
their \emph{property I}: a hierarchical system built from the ordered payoff
partition and the coalitions tight at the candidate has only the zero
feasible direction.  Under the standard antiduality between Lmax and Lmin,
this becomes a property-I characterization of Lmin using the reversed payoff
partition and complement-tight coalitions
\citep[Lemma~3]{Mauri2018}.  The lemma below is not claimed as a new
characterization of Lmin.  It records the quantitative certificate needed
here: one nonnegative combination of the original tight-coalition incidence
vectors that is regular on an entire richer suffix and simultaneously
strictly deficient at every poorer player.  The explicit LP dual and backward
combination show how that certificate follows from the same lexicographic
optimality structure; the subsequent projection to rich-side traces is the
additional step used in this paper.

\begin{lemma}[strict richer-side cover]
\label{lem:lx-strict-richer-cover}
Let $\phi=\operatorname{Lmin}(v)$ and let a strict payoff boundary have
nonempty poorer side $L$ and richer suffix $H$.  There are nonnegative
weights $\lambda_T$ on proper coalitions tight at $\phi$ and a number
$\alpha>0$ such that, with
\[
 c_i:=\sum_{T\ni i}\lambda_T,
\]
one has
\[
 c_h=\alpha\quad(h\in H),\qquad
 c_i<\alpha\quad(i\in L).                        \tag{SC}
 \label{eq:lx-strict-richer-cover}
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
At a progressive Lmin stage, hold all earlier coordinates fixed, retain the
common floor on the current and later players, and maximize one player $i$ in
the current stratum.  Because $i$ is finalized at that level, the optimum is
$\phi_i$: if a feasible point raised $i$, a short segment from $\phi$ toward
it would keep every later stratum strictly above the current level and would
lexicographically improve the current tied block.

Here is the dual calculation explicitly.  Put $P$ for the earlier players,
$\ell=\phi_i$, and minimize $-x_i$ subject to
\[
 v(S)-x(S)\leq0\quad(\emptyset\ne S\subsetneq N),
 \qquad \ell-x_q\leq0\quad(q\notin P),
\]
together with $x_p=\phi_p$ for $p\in P$ and $x(N)=v(N)$.  Let
$\lambda_S,\mu_q\geq0$ be the two families of inequality multipliers, let
$\nu_i$ be the free efficiency multiplier, and put
$w_q=\sum_{S\ni q}\lambda_S$.  Stationarity at $q\notin P$ is
\[
 -\mathbf1_{\{q=i\}}-w_q-\mu_q+\nu_i=0.             \tag{SC-D}
 \label{eq:lx-strict-cover-dual}
\]
Every later player's floor is strictly inactive at $\phi$, hence
$w_q=\nu_i$ there.  At the target,
$w_i=\nu_i-1-\mu_i\leq\nu_i-1$, and every other player in the current
stratum has $w_q=\nu_i-\mu_q\leq\nu_i$.  Complementary slackness restricts
positive $\lambda_S$ to $\phi$-tight coalitions.  Earlier coordinates carry
free equality multipliers and impose no load restriction.  Finally
$w_i\geq0$ implies $\nu_i\geq1$.

Write the poorer payoff strata as $B_1,\ldots,B_j$, from lowest to highest.
For each $t$, add the individual-pinning duals of all $i\in B_t$ and call the
resulting cover $D_t$.  It has a common load $\eta_t$ on every later player,
and every $i\in B_t$ has load at most $\eta_t-1$: its own dual contributes
the unit deficit and every other dual in the stratum contributes at most its
common later load.  Loads on earlier strata are finite but unrestricted.

Combine the $D_t$ backwards.  Start with any positive multiple of $D_j$.
Suppose a combination of $D_{t+1},\ldots,D_j$ has common load $A$ on $H$
and finite loads $r_i$ on $B_t$.  Adding $M D_t$ changes the common rich load
to $A+M\eta_t$, while the load on $i\in B_t$ is at most
$r_i+M(\eta_t-1)$.  Its rich-side deficit is at least
$A-r_i+M>0$ for all sufficiently large $M$.  Players in later poorer strata
receive the same added load as $H$, so their already strict deficits are
preserved.  Backward induction gives one cover satisfying
\eqref{eq:lx-strict-richer-cover}.  Since every target load is nonnegative
and at most $\nu_i-1$, each $\nu_i\geq1$, so the final $\alpha$ is positive.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[laminar strict-cover domain]
\label{thm:lx-laminar-strict-cover}
Let $v$ be exact, let $\phi\in\core(v)$, and let
$\varnothing\ne H\subsetneq N$.  Suppose nonnegative weights on proper
$\phi$-tight coalitions have a common positive load on $H$, strictly smaller
load on every player in $N\setminus H$, and laminar positive support.  Then
$H$ is tight at $\phi$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $\mathcal F$ be the positive support of the cover.  For each $h\in H$,
choose an inclusion-minimal $M_h\in\mathcal F$ containing $h$.  If
$i\in M_h\cap L$, every supported coalition containing $h$ also contains
$i$: it intersects $M_h$, so laminarity makes the two sets nested, and
minimality rules out a strict supported subset of $M_h$ containing $h$.
The load of $i$ would therefore be at least the common load of $h$, contrary
to the strict poorer-side deficit.  Hence $M_h\subseteq H$.

The inclusion-maximal distinct members of $\{M_h:h\in H\}$ are pairwise
disjoint and cover $H$.  They form a partition $\mathcal P$ of $H$ by
$\phi$-tight coalitions.  If $\mathcal P=\{H\}$, the conclusion is immediate.
Otherwise, give each $B\in\mathcal P$ weight one, give $H$
weight minus one, and give $N$ weight one.  The incidence sum is
$\mathbf1_N$, with $H$ the only negative term, so exact balancedness yields
\[
 \sum_{B\in\mathcal P}v(B)-v(H)+v(N)\leq v(N).
\]
Tightness of the blocks gives $\phi(H)\leq v(H)$, while core feasibility
gives the reverse inequality.  Thus $H$ is tight.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[laminar Lmin domain]
If a singleton-normalized exact game admits a laminar strict richer-side
cover at every strict Lmin boundary, it is exact partition witnessed at Lmin.
In particular, this holds when its complete proper Lmin-tight family is
laminar.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-strict-richer-cover} and
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-laminar-strict-cover} at every boundary.  If the complete
tight family is laminar, the lemma's cover has laminar support.
\end{proof}

Thus every slack Lmin suffix forces crossing coalitions in the support of
\emph{every} strict richer-side cover.  This is a boundary-local,
dimension-free domain theorem, not merely a cardinality exclusion.  The
known five-player failure escapes exactly through its two crossing protector
coalitions.  A standalone proof and finite audit are
\begin{center}
\small\path{paper3/LAMINAR_LMIN_DOMAIN_THEOREM.md},\\[-2pt]
\small\path{src/verify_laminar_lmin_domain.py},\\[-2pt]
\small\path{results/laminar_lmin_domain.json}.
\end{center}

The laminar condition admits a substantially broader uncrossing form.  Say
that $v$ is \emph{Lmin-locally supermodular} if, for
$\phi=\operatorname{Lmin}(v)$, every pair $A,B$ tight at $\phi$ satisfies
\[
 v(A)+v(B)\leq v(A\cap B)+v(A\cup B).                \tag{LSM}
 \label{eq:lx-local-supermodularity}
\]
Unlike convexity, this condition is imposed only on coalitions binding at one
canonical core allocation.

\begin{theorem}[exact-face uncrossing]
\label{thm:lx-local-supermodular-domain}
Let $v$ be exact, let $\phi\in\core(v)$, and let
$\varnothing\ne H\subsetneq N$.  Suppose nonnegative weights on proper
$\phi$-tight coalitions have a common positive load on $H$ and strictly
smaller load on every player outside $H$.  If the $\phi$-tight family is
closed under union and intersection, then $H$ is tight at $\phi$.
Conversely, if $H$ is slack, there are crossing $\phi$-tight coalitions $A,B$
with
\[
 v(A)+v(B)-v(A\cap B)-v(A\cup B)
 =s_\phi(A\cap B)+s_\phi(A\cup B)>0.                 \tag{UD}
 \label{eq:lx-uncrossing-defect}
\]
The pair may be chosen from a minimum-energy representation of a strict
cover with the prescribed player loads.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
First observe that if tight $A,B$ satisfy
\eqref{eq:lx-local-supermodularity}, then both $A\cap B$ and $A\cup B$ are
tight.  Indeed,
\[
 v(A)+v(B)\leq v(A\cap B)+v(A\cup B)
 \leq\phi(A\cap B)+\phi(A\cup B)
 =\phi(A)+\phi(B)=v(A)+v(B),
\]
so all inequalities are equalities and both nonnegative slacks vanish.

Retain the complete player-load vector $c$ of the assumed cover.  Among all
nonnegative representations of $c$ by nonempty
$\phi$-tight coalitions, now allowing $N$, minimize
\[
 \mathcal E(\lambda):=\sum_S\lambda_S|S|(n-|S|).      \tag{UE}
 \label{eq:lx-uncrossing-energy}
\]
The representation set is nonempty and compact: after the empty coalition is
discarded, every coefficient is bounded by a coordinate of $c$.

If positive-support coalitions $A,B$ cross, put
$\delta=\min\{\lambda_A,\lambda_B\}$.  Subtract $\delta$ from their weights
and add it to $A\cap B$ and $A\cup B$, discarding an empty intersection.
The incidence identity
$\mathbf1_A+\mathbf1_B=\mathbf1_{A\cap B}+\mathbf1_{A\cup B}$ preserves
$c$, while tight-pair closure preserves feasibility.  But the energy falls by
\[
 2\delta|A\setminus B|\,|B\setminus A|>0,             \tag{UE-D}
 \label{eq:lx-uncrossing-energy-drop}
\]
a contradiction.  Thus the minimizing support is laminar.  If it contains
$N$ with weight $\gamma$, remove that term.  Every load is at least $\gamma$;
the residual rich load is positive because each poorer load was strictly
below the original rich load, and all rich--poor gaps are unchanged.  The
remaining proper support is a laminar strict cover, so
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-laminar-strict-cover} makes $H$ tight.  Applying this at
every boundary proves the first assertion.

For the converse, minimize \eqref{eq:lx-uncrossing-energy} without assuming
uncrossing closure.  If $H$ is slack, the minimizing support
cannot be laminar, by Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-laminar-strict-cover}; choose a
crossing positive pair $A,B$.  Their intersection and union cannot both be
tight, or the same uncrossing move would lower the energy.  Modularity of
$\phi$ and tightness of $A,B$ give the equality in
\eqref{eq:lx-uncrossing-defect}; at least one derived-set slack is positive.
\end{proof}

The broken-diamond obstruction has a quantitative conservation law.  For a
core point $\phi$, write
\[
 D_\phi(A,B):=v(A)+v(B)-v(A\cap B)-v(A\cup B).
\]

\begin{theorem}[uncrossing-defect budget]
\label{thm:lx-uncrossing-defect-budget}
Let $v$ be exact, let $\phi\in\core(v)$, and let
$\varnothing\ne H\subsetneq N$.  Suppose an integer multiset of proper
$\phi$-tight coalitions has common load $k>0$ on $H$ and load at most $k-1$
on $N\setminus H$.  Repeatedly uncross a positive pair $A_t,B_t$, with
$\delta_t$ equal to the smaller multiplicity, until the support is laminar.
From the final multiplicities $\mu$, extract the partition $\mathcal P$ of
$H$ formed by the maximal minimal supported sets containing its players, and
put $m=\min_{B\in\mathcal P}\mu_B$.  Then
\[
 0\leq m s_\phi(H)
 \leq \sum_S\mu_Ss_\phi(S)
 =\sum_t\delta_tD_\phi(A_t,B_t).                     \tag{UDB}
 \label{eq:lx-uncrossing-defect-budget}
\]
The procedure terminates after finitely many steps.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Each uncrossing preserves player loads and lowers the integer energy
$\sum_S\lambda_S|S|(n-|S|)$ by
$2\delta_t|A_t\setminus B_t|\,|B_t\setminus A_t|>0$.
Thus the procedure terminates with laminar support.  The minimal-set argument
from Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-laminar-strict-cover} applies without tightness of
the final sets: if a minimal set containing $h\in H$ also contained
$i\notin H$, laminarity would make the final load of $i$ at least $k$.
Hence the maximal minimal sets form a partition $\mathcal P$ of $H$.

Initially the weighted slack is zero.  One uncrossing changes it by
\[
 \delta_t\{s_\phi(A_t\cap B_t)+s_\phi(A_t\cup B_t)
            -s_\phi(A_t)-s_\phi(B_t)\}
 =\delta_tD_\phi(A_t,B_t),
\]
so telescoping proves the equality in
\eqref{eq:lx-uncrossing-defect-budget}.  Final slacks are nonnegative and
every block of $\mathcal P$ has multiplicity at least $m$.  Exact balancedness
applied with weight one on the partition blocks, minus one on $H$, and plus
one on $N$ gives
$v(H)\geq\sum_{B\in\mathcal P}v(B)$, hence
$s_\phi(H)\leq\sum_{B\in\mathcal P}s_\phi(B)$.  Multiplying by $m$ proves
the inequalities.
\end{proof}

Thus local supermodularity defects do not merely witness failure: their signed
cumulative total exactly equals the weighted slack created by uncrossing and
must finance the target-face slack.  Any rational strict cover can be scaled
to the integer form above; in particular, the strict-cover polyhedron at an
Lmin boundary is rational.  The sharp five-player cover has cumulative defect
two and target slack one.  The three-column seven-player cover uncrosses in
two steps with defects $3$ and $3/2$, again funding unit target slack.  A
standalone proof and exact audit are
\begin{center}
\small\path{paper3/UNCROSSING_DEFECT_BUDGET.md},\\[-2pt]
\small\path{src/verify_uncrossing_defect_budget.py},\\[-2pt]
\small\path{results/uncrossing_defect_budget.json}.
\end{center}

The entire two-column obstruction tier has a normal form and a sharp slack
budget.

\begin{theorem}[two-column exterior budget]
\label{thm:lx-two-column-normal-form}
Let $v$ be singleton-normalized and exact, let
$\phi=\operatorname{Lmin}(v)$, and let $H$ be a slack cumulative richer
suffix. Suppose a strict $\phi$-tight cover of $H$ has exactly two
positive-support proper coalitions. Then there are nonempty, pairwise
disjoint $X,Y,R$ such that
\[
 N=H\mathbin{\dot\cup}X\mathbin{\dot\cup}Y\mathbin{\dot\cup}R,
 \qquad A=H\cup X,\qquad B=H\cup Y,
\]
where $A,B$ are the two support coalitions. Moreover
\[
 0<s_\phi(H)\leq s_\phi(R).                         \tag{ESB}
 \label{eq:lx-exterior-slack-budget}
\]
Thus every two-column failure is an oriented broken diamond, and its exterior
coalition finances the failed exact face.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write the two positive weights as $\lambda,\mu$ and their common load on
$H$ as $\alpha$. Since $H$ is slack, Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-local-supermodular-domain}
and its laminar endpoint imply that the support coalitions cross. Their four
Venn-cell loads are $\lambda+\mu,\lambda,\mu,0$. Since $\alpha$ is one of
these four loads, $\alpha\leq\lambda+\mu$. The nonempty intersection cannot
therefore contain an outside player, whose load must be strictly below
$\alpha$. Hence it lies in and meets $H$, so $\alpha=\lambda+\mu$. Equality of the loads on
$H$ and strict inequality outside now force $A\cap B=H$; crossing gives
nonempty arms $X,Y$ outside $H$.

Put $s=s_\phi(H)$ and choose, by exactness, $z\in\core(v)$ with
$z(H)=v(H)$. For $d=z-\phi$, tightness of $A,B$ gives
$d(X),d(Y)\geq s$. Efficiency yields
\[
 d(R)=s-d(X)-d(Y)\leq-s.
\]
Core feasibility gives $z(R)\geq v(R)$, equivalently
$d(R)\geq-s_\phi(R)$, proving \eqref{eq:lx-exterior-slack-budget} and also
$R\ne\emptyset$. This is the slack form of the exact-balanced identity
\[
 \mathbf1_A+\mathbf1_B+\mathbf1_R-\mathbf1_H=\mathbf1_N. \qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

The normal form has a sharp converse. Let
$N=C\mathbin{\dot\cup}X\mathbin{\dot\cup}Y\mathbin{\dot\cup}R$, with all
four blocks nonempty, put $A=C\cup X$, $B=C\cup Y$, and write
$c=|C|$, $x=|X|$, $y=|Y|$, $r=|R|$.

\begin{theorem}[binary broken-diamond realization]
\label{thm:lx-binary-diamond-realization}
Choose payoff levels $\rho>\sigma>0$ and suppose
\[
 c\geq2\qquad\hbox{or}\qquad \sigma r\geq\rho.      \tag{CAP}
 \label{eq:lx-binary-capacity}
\]
Give members of $C$ payoff $\rho$ and all other players payoff $\sigma$,
obtaining $\phi$. Let
\[
 K=\{z\geq0:z(N)=\phi(N),\ z(A)\geq\phi(A),\ z(B)\geq\phi(B)\},
 \qquad v(S)=\min_{z\in K}z(S).
\]
Then $v$ is singleton-normalized and exact, $\core(v)=K$, and
$\operatorname{Lmin}(v)=\phi$. Coalitions $A,B$ are tight, while
\[
 s_\phi(C)=\min\{\rho c,\sigma r\}>0,
 \qquad
 s_\phi(A\cup B)=\sigma\min\{x,y\}>0.              \tag{BDR}
 \label{eq:lx-binary-diamond-slacks}
\]

For this lower-envelope family, condition \eqref{eq:lx-binary-capacity} is
also necessary for singleton normalization when $c=1$. Across all exact
games, the first two-column Lmin failure has five players, attained at
$\rho=2,\sigma=1$ by exactly the shapes
$(c,x,y,r)=(2,1,1,1)$ and $(1,1,1,2)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The point $\phi$ is feasible and makes both displayed constraints tight.
The lower-envelope two-inclusion argument gives $\core(v)=K$ and exactness.
Every coordinate has minimum zero. The $\sigma$ units on a player in $X,Y$,
or $R$ can be moved to $C$. A member of $C$ can transfer its $\rho$ units to
another member when $c\geq2$. If $c=1$, zeroing the member of $C$, adding
$\rho$ units in total to each of $X,Y$, and removing $\rho$ units in total
from $R$ is feasible exactly when $\sigma r\geq\rho$.
Thus singleton values are zero.

The unit weights on $A,B$ load $C$ twice, $X\cup Y$ once, and $R$ zero
times. More directly, if a core point has every coordinate at least $\sigma$,
write it as $\sigma\mathbf1_N+w$ with $w\geq0$. The two inequalities and
efficiency give
\[
 w(C)+w(X),\ w(C)+w(Y)\geq(\rho-\sigma)c,
 \qquad w(N)=(\rho-\sigma)c.
\]
Hence $w$ vanishes on $X\cup Y\cup R$. The remaining total on $C$ is
max--min equalized at $\rho$ per player, proving $\operatorname{Lmin}(v)=\phi$.

At the block-aggregate level the constraints are
$z(C)+z(X)\geq\rho c+\sigma x$ and
$z(C)+z(Y)\geq\rho c+\sigma y$. Efficiency gives
\[
 v(C)=\max\{0,\rho c-\sigma r\},\qquad
 v(C\cup X\cup Y)=\rho c+\sigma\max\{x,y\},
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:lx-binary-diamond-slacks}.

If $c=1$, any feasible point zeroing $C$ must increase each arm total by at
least $\rho$. Efficiency therefore decreases $R$ by at least $\rho$, proving
necessity of $\sigma r\geq\rho$. For the general lower bound, four players in
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-two-column-normal-form} make both $H$ and $R$ singletons.
Normalization turns \eqref{eq:lx-exterior-slack-budget} into
$\phi(H)\leq\phi(R)$, contradicting the strict payoff boundary. At five
players, placing the extra member in an arm leaves $H,R$ singleton and gives
the same contradiction. Hence only a second member of $H$ or of $R$ can
occur at the threshold; the displayed levels realize both possibilities.
\end{proof}

Thus the four/five threshold is a slack-financing threshold: crossing needs
the three cells $C,X,Y$, exact balancedness requires the exterior cell $R$,
and singleton exactness requires one further unit of target-or-exterior
capacity. The proof is human and dimension-free. Standalone versions and
exact parameter audits are
\begin{center}
\small\path{paper3/TWO_COLUMN_LMIN_OBSTRUCTION.md},\\[-2pt]
\small\path{src/verify_two_column_lmin_obstruction.py},\\[-2pt]
\small\path{results/two_column_lmin_obstruction.json},\\[-2pt]
\small\path{paper3/BINARY_BROKEN_DIAMOND_REALIZATION.md},\\[-2pt]
\small\path{src/verify_binary_broken_diamond_realization.py},\\[-2pt]
\small\path{results/binary_broken_diamond_realization.json}.
\end{center}

\begin{corollary}[Lmin local-supermodularity domain]
\label{cor:lx-local-supermodular-lmin}
Every singleton-normalized exact Lmin-locally supermodular game is an exact
partition game witnessed at Lmin.  Conversely, every slack cumulative richer
suffix at Lmin yields a broken tight diamond satisfying
\eqref{eq:lx-uncrossing-defect}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
At every strict Lmin boundary,
Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-strict-richer-cover} supplies the cover required by
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-local-supermodular-domain}.  As observed before the
theorem, local supermodularity makes the tight family closed under union and
intersection.  Apply the theorem at every boundary.  Its converse gives the
final assertion.
\end{proof}

The exact-face theorem does not require normalization or an egalitarian
allocation; these enter only to generate its cover automatically.  Its Lmin
corollary subsumes the laminar domain and the established convex-game domain
but is stated only in terms of local behavior on the Lmin-tight skeleton.
The inclusion beyond convex games is strict.  For example, take
the lower envelope of the four common-total allocations
\[
 (0,4,6,5,0),\quad(5,0,2,7,1),\quad
 (3,0,0,11,1),\quad(2,2,6,0,5).
\]
It is singleton-normalized and exact, has
$\operatorname{Lmin}(v)=(5/2,5/2,10/3,10/3,10/3)$, and its only nonempty
proper Lmin-tight coalition is the richer suffix $\{3,4,5\}$.  Hence it is
Lmin-locally supermodular and exact partition.  It is not convex: the pair
$\{1,2,5\},\{1,3,5\}$ violates supermodularity by four.

In the sharp five-player failure, the tight protectors
$\{1,3,5\}$ and $\{3,4,5\}$ have intersection $\{3,5\}$ and union
$\{1,3,4,5\}$, each with slack one, so their defect in
\eqref{eq:lx-uncrossing-defect} is exactly two.  Their support has only two
columns and is therefore balanced and totally balanced; this disproves any
extension based only on balanced incidence.  A standalone proof, hostile
review prompt, and exact audit are
\begin{center}
\small\path{paper3/LMIN_UNCROSSING_DOMAIN_THEOREM.md},\\[-2pt]
\small\path{paper3/LMIN_UNCROSSING_EXTERNAL_REVIEW_PROMPT.md},\\[-2pt]
\small\path{src/verify_lmin_uncrossing_domain.py},\\[-2pt]
\small\path{results/lmin_uncrossing_domain.json}.
\end{center}

The preceding certificates also show that the entire characteristic function
away from the Lmin-tight coalitions is dispensable.  This gives a canonical
support-independent reduction before any trace classification is attempted.

\begin{theorem}[Lmin-tight-skeleton retraction]
\label{thm:lx-tight-skeleton-retraction}
Let $v$ be a singleton-normalized exact game, put
$\phi=\operatorname{Lmin}(v)$, and let
\[
 \mathcal T_\phi:=\{S:\varnothing\ne S\subsetneq N,
                    \ v(S)=\phi(S)\}.
\]
Define
\[
 K_\phi:=\{x\in\mathbb R_+^N:x(N)=v(N),\quad
                 x(S)\geq\phi(S)\ (S\in\mathcal T_\phi)\},       \tag{SK-1}
 \label{eq:lx-tight-skeleton-polytope}
\]
and let
\[
 (R_\phi v)(A):=\min_{x\in K_\phi}x(A).                         \tag{SK-2}
 \label{eq:lx-tight-skeleton-game}
\]
Then $R_\phi v$ is singleton-normalized and exact,
$\core(R_\phi v)=K_\phi$, and
\[
 R_\phi v\leq v,\qquad
 \operatorname{Lmin}(R_\phi v)=\phi,\qquad
 (R_\phi v)(A)=\phi(A)\Longleftrightarrow v(A)=\phi(A).          \tag{SK-3}
 \label{eq:lx-tight-skeleton-properties}
\]
Thus the complete Lmin-tight/slack pattern, including exact-partition
status at every cumulative richer suffix, is preserved.  The operation is
idempotent.  For rational input, a common positive scaling makes
$R_\phi v$ integral.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Exactness and singleton normalization give $\core(v)\subseteq K_\phi$.
Consequently, for every coalition $A$,
\[
 (R_\phi v)(A)\leq\min_{x\in\core(v)}x(A)=v(A).                  \tag{SK-4}
 \label{eq:lx-tight-skeleton-domination}
\]
If $A\in\mathcal T_\phi$, its defining inequality and the feasible point
$\phi$ give $(R_\phi v)(A)=\phi(A)=v(A)$.  For a singleton,
\eqref{eq:lx-tight-skeleton-domination} and nonnegativity give value zero;
efficiency handles $N$.  If a proper $A$ is not in $\mathcal T_\phi$, then
$(R_\phi v)(A)\leq v(A)<\phi(A)$.  This proves the last equivalence in
\eqref{eq:lx-tight-skeleton-properties}.

Every point of $K_\phi$ satisfies all inequalities of its lower envelope,
so it lies in $\core(R_\phi v)$.  Conversely, a core point of $R_\phi v$ is
efficient, is nonnegative because the new singleton values are zero, and
satisfies the inequalities indexed by $\mathcal T_\phi$ at their just-proved
values $\phi(S)$.  It lies in $K_\phi$.  Hence the two sets coincide.
Every coalition minimum is attained on the compact polytope, proving
exactness.

Write the payoff strata of $\phi$ as $B_1,\ldots,B_k$, with increasing
levels.  For $j<k$, put
$L_j=B_1\mathbin{\dot\cup}\cdots\mathbin{\dot\cup}B_j$ and
$H_j=N\setminus L_j$.  Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-strict-richer-cover} supplies
weights on $\mathcal T_\phi$ with common load $\alpha_j>0$ on $H_j$ and
loads $d_i<\alpha_j$ on $L_j$.  Summing their defining inequalities in
$K_\phi$ and eliminating $x(H_j)$ by efficiency gives
\[
 \sum_{i\in L_j}(\alpha_j-d_i)(x_i-\phi_i)\leq0.                \tag{SK-5}
 \label{eq:lx-tight-skeleton-gate}
\]
All coefficients are positive.  At the first progressive leximin stage,
\eqref{eq:lx-tight-skeleton-gate} for $j=1$ pins every member of $B_1$ at
its value in $\phi$.  Inductively, after the earlier blocks are fixed, the
$j$th inequality reduces to a positive weighted sum over $B_j$ and pins that
block.  Efficiency pins the last block.  Therefore $\phi$ remains Lmin.

The tight set and total are now unchanged, so a second application uses the
same polytope and lower envelope; this proves idempotence.  Rational data
define a rational polytope and rational coalition minima, whose finitely many
denominators can be cleared.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[certificate-only hypergraph realization]
\label{cor:lx-tight-skeleton-realization}
Let $\phi\in\mathbb R_+^N$, let $\mathcal T$ be any family of proper
nonempty coalitions, and define
\[
 K(\mathcal T,\phi):=\{x\in\mathbb R_+^N:x(N)=\phi(N),\quad
                         x(T)\geq\phi(T)\ (T\in\mathcal T)\}.
\]
Write the increasing payoff strata of $\phi$ as $B_1,\ldots,B_k$.  Suppose

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $\min_{x\in K(\mathcal T,\phi)}x_i=0$ for every player $i$;
\item for every $j<k$, nonnegative weights on $\mathcal T$ have common
load $\alpha_j>0$ on
$H_j=N\setminus(B_1\mathbin{\dot\cup}\cdots\mathbin{\dot\cup}B_j)$
and load strictly below $\alpha_j$ on every player outside $H_j$.
\end{enumerate}
Then the lower envelope of $K(\mathcal T,\phi)$ is a
singleton-normalized exact game with Lmin $\phi$, and every member of
$\mathcal T$ is tight at $\phi$.  Its proper tight set is exactly
$\mathcal T$ if and only if
\[
 \min_{x\in K(\mathcal T,\phi)}x(A)<\phi(A)
 \quad\text{for every proper }A\notin\mathcal T.                 \tag{SK-6}
 \label{eq:lx-tight-skeleton-no-new-tight}
\]
Conversely, the complete Lmin-tight family of every
singleton-normalized exact game satisfies (i), (ii), and
\eqref{eq:lx-tight-skeleton-no-new-tight}.  Thus normalized exact Lmin
skeletons have a certificate-only characterization involving no unspecified
coalition values.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Condition (i), the listed inequalities, and the feasible point $\phi$ prove
that the lower envelope has singleton values zero and has
$w(T)=\phi(T)$ for $T\in\mathcal T$.  The two-inclusion argument of
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-tight-skeleton-retraction} gives
$\core(w)=K(\mathcal T,\phi)$ and exactness.  Condition (ii) gives
\eqref{eq:lx-tight-skeleton-gate} at every payoff boundary, so the same
progressive induction proves $\operatorname{Lmin}(w)=\phi$.
Condition~\eqref{eq:lx-tight-skeleton-no-new-tight} is exactly the assertion
that no omitted proper coalition is tight.  For the converse, apply
exactness to every singleton, Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-strict-richer-cover} to every
strict payoff boundary, and the definition of the complete tight family.
\end{proof}

The strict-cover ingredient follows from the published Lmin optimality
structure discussed above.  The map sending a balanced game to the unique
exact game with the same core is already known as its lower envelope or exact
cover; see, for example,
\citet{Schmeidler1972,StudenyKratochvil2022}.  The candidate new
claim is their exact idempotent combination: deleting every Lmin-slack
coalition constraint, then exactifying, preserves Lmin and the entire
coalitionwise tightness pattern.  We do not claim novelty for either
ingredient separately.  The standalone proof and an exact audit on three
unrelated families are
\begin{center}
\small\path{paper3/LMIN_TIGHT_SKELETON_RETRACTION.md},\\[-2pt]
\small\path{src/verify_lmin_tight_skeleton_retraction.py},\\[-2pt]
\small\path{results/lmin_tight_skeleton_retraction.json}.
\end{center}

\begin{theorem}[balanced-trace transport obstruction]
\label{thm:lx-balanced-trace-transport}
Let $v$ be exact, let $\phi=\operatorname{Lmin}(v)$, and let $H$ be a
cumulative richer suffix at a strict payoff boundary, with
$L=N\setminus H\ne\emptyset$.  If
\[
 s:=\phi(H)-v(H)>0,
\]
then there are a core point $z$, a displacement $\Delta=z-\phi$, and a
finite convex mixture of lifted normalized minimal balanced packets on $H$
with weights $\theta_q>0$ and poorer-player load vectors
$r^q\in\mathbb R_+^L$ such that
\begin{align}
 \Delta(H)&=-s,& \Delta(L)&=s,                         \tag{BT-1}\label{eq:lx-transport-direction}\\
 \min_{i\in L}\Delta_i&<0<&\max_{i\in L}\Delta_i,    \tag{BT-2}\label{eq:lx-transport-signs}\\
 -s+\sum_{i\in L}r_i^q\Delta_i&\geq0
     &&\text{for every packet }q,                     \tag{BT-3}\label{eq:lx-transport-packet}\\
 \sum_q\theta_q r_i^q&<1
     &&(i\in L),\qquad \sum_q\theta_q=1.             \tag{BT-4}\label{eq:lx-transport-average}
\end{align}
Thus every slack Lmin boundary requires a minimal balanced rich-side circuit
that transports the suffix slack through opposing gains and losses on the
poorer side.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Choose $z\in\core(v)$ exact on $H$.  Efficiency gives
\eqref{eq:lx-transport-direction}.  Apply
Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-strict-richer-cover}, normalize its rich load to one, and
discard cover weight on coalitions disjoint from $H$.  The remaining cover
still has unit load on every member of $H$ and has loads $c_i<1$ on $L$.
Summing $z(T)-\phi(T)\geq0$ over its tight coalitions gives
\[
 -s+\sum_{i\in L}c_i\Delta_i\geq0.                   \tag{BT-C}
 \label{eq:lx-transport-cover}
\]
If every $\Delta_i$ on $L$ were nonnegative, the left side would be strictly
less than $-s+\Delta(L)=0$.  Since $\Delta(L)=s>0$, there is also a positive
entry.  This proves \eqref{eq:lx-transport-signs}.

Project the remaining tight coalitions to their nonempty traces on $H$.
Their weights form a point of the bounded polytope
\[
 \mathcal P_H=\left\{\beta\geq0:
       \sum_{\emptyset\ne R\subseteq H}\beta_R\mathbf1_R
       =\mathbf1_H\right\}.
\]
(Boundedness follows from $\sum_R\beta_R\leq|H|$.)  Its extreme points are
exactly the unique normalized balancing weights on minimal balanced
collections: a nonminimal support contains a balanced subcollection and
admits a nontrivial feasible perturbation, while a minimal balanced support
has unique weights.  Thus the projected cover is a convex combination of
normalized minimal balanced packets; see also
\citet{LaplaceMermoudEtAl2025}.

For each trace $R$ in a packet, split among the original coalitions having
that trace using their conditional cover weights.  Taking the product
distribution across the finitely many trace slots refines the decomposition
into a finite convex mixture of lifted deterministic packets.  Indeed, after
averaging first over the product choices and then over packets, the weight of
an original coalition $T$ is its trace weight times its conditional share,
hence exactly the original $\lambda_T$.  The lift therefore preserves the
entire average cover, not only its rich projection.

For a lifted packet $q$, let $r_i^q$ be its total packet weight on coalitions
containing poorer player $i$.  Its rich trace has unit load, so summing
$z(T)-\phi(T)\geq0$ with its packet weights proves
\eqref{eq:lx-transport-packet}.  Averaging the lifted packets recovers the
original poorer loads $c_i<1$, which is \eqref{eq:lx-transport-average}.
\end{proof}

The preceding theorem forces a fractional hypergraph obstruction whenever the
poor-side transport has a unique gaining coordinate.  This gives a
dimension-free condition on the actual tight-coalition geometry, rather than
only on the number of players.

\begin{definition}[active trace polytope]
\label{def:lx-active-trace-polytope}
At a strict Lmin boundary $(L,H)$, let
\[
 \mathcal A_\phi(H):=\{T\cap H:\ T\subsetneq N,\
   \ \phi(T)=v(T),\ T\cap H\ne\varnothing\}
\]
and define its fractional exact-cover polytope
\[
 \mathcal Q_\phi(H):=
 \left\{\beta\in\mathbb R_+^{\mathcal A_\phi(H)}:
   \sum_{R\in\mathcal A_\phi(H)}\beta_R\mathbf1_R
      =\mathbf1_H\right\}.                         \tag{AT}
 \label{eq:lx-active-trace-polytope}
\]
Call the boundary \emph{trace-integral} if every vertex of
$\mathcal Q_\phi(H)$ is integral.  An integral point of
\eqref{eq:lx-active-trace-polytope} is a set partition of $H$.
\end{definition}

Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-strict-richer-cover} makes
$\mathcal Q_\phi(H)$ nonempty, and it is bounded because every trace is
nonempty.

\begin{theorem}[integral-trace obstruction]
\label{thm:lx-integral-trace-obstruction}
Let $v$ be exact, let $\phi=\operatorname{Lmin}(v)$, and let $(L,H)$ be a
strict payoff boundary.  Suppose the exact $H$-face contains a point
$z\in\core(v)$ such that
\[
 z(H)=v(H),\qquad
 \bigl|\{i\in L:z_i>\phi_i\}\bigr|\leq1.            \tag{IT}
 \label{eq:lx-integral-trace-witness}
\]
If the boundary is trace-integral, then $H$ is tight at $\phi$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Suppose instead that $s:=\phi(H)-v(H)>0$ and put $\Delta=z-\phi$.
Normalize the cover from Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-strict-richer-cover} to unit load
on $H$, discard coalitions disjoint from $H$, and write $c_i<1$ for its loads
on $L$.  The argument proving \eqref{eq:lx-transport-signs}, applied to this
chosen $z$, shows that $\Delta_L$ has both signs.  Since
$\Delta(L)=s>0$, condition \eqref{eq:lx-integral-trace-witness} therefore
gives a unique $b\in L$ with $\Delta_b>0$, while
$\Delta_i\leq0$ for $i\ne b$.

The projected cover lies in $\mathcal Q_\phi(H)$.  Trace integrality
decomposes it into a convex mixture of integral points, hence set partitions
of $H$.  Lift every partition to deterministic original tight coalitions by
the same conditional product construction used in
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-balanced-trace-transport}.  In a lifted partition packet
the poorer-player loads $r_i$ are nonnegative integers, and its tight
coalition inequalities at $z$ give
\[
 -s+\sum_{i\in L}r_i\Delta_i\geq0.                  \tag{IT-P}
 \label{eq:lx-integral-trace-packet}
\]
If $r_b=0$, the left side is at most $-s$, so every packet has $r_b\geq1$.
Averaging gives $c_b\geq1$, contradicting $c_b<1$.
\end{proof}

Equivalently, if a trace-integral boundary is slack, then every core point
exact on $H$ has at least two gaining poorer coordinates; the sign argument
also gives at least one losing poorer coordinate.  Thus trace-integral slack
requires at least three poorer players.  This sign-complexity statement is
independent of $|H|$.

\begin{corollary}[odd-trace-cycle necessity]
\label{cor:lx-odd-trace-cycle}
With exactly two poorer players, every slack richer Lmin suffix has a
nonintegral active trace polytope.  Consequently its player-by-trace incidence
matrix is not balanced and contains an odd-order square submatrix with exactly
two ones in every row and column.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Exactness supplies $z$ exact on $H$.  Under slack,
applying the normalized strict cover to this $z$, exactly as in the proof of
\eqref{eq:lx-transport-signs}, makes its two poor displacements have opposite
signs.  Thus \eqref{eq:lx-integral-trace-witness} holds.  Theorem
\ref{thm:lx-integral-trace-obstruction} rules out integrality.  A balanced
zero--one matrix has an integral set-partitioning polytope
\citep{Berge1972}; taking the contrapositive and the defining forbidden
submatrix characterization of balanced matrices gives the odd trace cycle.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[safe rooted tight cover]
\label{def:lx-safe-rooted-tight-cover}
At a strict boundary with two poorer players $L=\{a,b\}$ and richer side
$H$, let $\mathcal T$ be any finite family of proper $\phi$-tight
coalitions.  A vector $y\in\mathbb Z_+^{\mathcal T}$ is a \emph{safe rooted
tight cover} if, for some $d\in H$ and integer $k\geq1$, its incidence loads
satisfy
\[
 \sum_{T\in\mathcal T}y_T\mathbf1_{T\cap H}
   =\mathbf1_H+k e_d,
 \qquad
 p:=\sum_{T\in\mathcal T}y_T\mathbf1_{T\cap L}
   \in\{0,1\}^{L}\setminus\{0\}.                  \tag{SRC}
 \label{eq:lx-safe-rooted-tight-cover}
\]
Equivalently, the affine semigroup generated by the lifted tight-coalition
columns meets a rooted exact-cover fibre while loading each poor player at
most once and at least one poor player once.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[exact-face rooted-semigroup obstruction]
\label{thm:lx-rooted-semigroup-obstruction}
Let $v$ be singleton-normalized and exact, let $\phi\in\core(v)$, and let
$(L,H)$ be a strict payoff boundary with $|L|=2$.  No family of proper
$\phi$-tight coalitions admits a safe rooted tight cover.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Apply Corollary~\ref{cor:lx-exact-face-overload} with $D=\{d\}$ and $q=k$.
Singleton normalization makes $s_\phi(\{d\})=\phi_d$.  If both poor players
are loaded, its right side is zero.  If exactly one is loaded and $j$ is
omitted, its right side is $\phi_j$.  The strict boundary gives
$k\phi_d>\phi_j\geq0$ in either case, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

The singleton root is the smallest instance of a more general overload
footprint left by alternating around an odd trace cycle.

\begin{lemma}[odd-cycle overload footprint]
\label{lem:lx-odd-cycle-overload-footprint}
Let $v$ be singleton-normalized and exact, let $\phi\in\core(v)$, and let a
strict payoff boundary have richer side $H$ and poorer side
$L=\{a,b\}$.  Suppose an odd family of proper $\phi$-tight coalitions has,
on a row set $R\subseteq H$, the cycle incidence matrix: after cyclic
indexing, each cycle row lies in its two incident columns.  Select an
alternating near-perfect cover, so its load on $R$ is
$\mathbf1_R+e_d$ for the duplicated cycle row $d$.  Suppose its load on
every $h\in H\setminus R$ is either one or two, and put
\[
 F:=\{d\}\cup
    \{h\in H\setminus R:\text{the selected load on $h$ is two}\}.
 \tag{OC-F}\label{eq:lx-odd-cycle-footprint}
\]
If the selected poor incidence is
$p\in\{0,1\}^{\{a,b\}}\setminus\{0\}$, then
\[
 s_\phi(F)\leq\sum_{i\in\{a,b\}:p_i=0}\phi_i.       \tag{OC-B}
 \label{eq:lx-odd-cycle-footprint-budget}
\]
In particular, if both poor players are loaded then $F$ is $\phi$-tight.
If $R=H$, such an alternating selection is impossible.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By construction, the selected rich incidence is
$\mathbf1_H+\mathbf1_F$, while its poor incidence is zero--one and nonzero.
Apply Corollary~\ref{cor:lx-exact-face-overload} with $q=1$.  This gives
\eqref{eq:lx-odd-cycle-footprint-budget}.  If $R=H$, then $F=\{d\}$ and
singleton normalization gives $s_\phi(D)=\phi_d$.  If both poor players are
loaded, the right side is zero.  If exactly one is loaded and $j$ is omitted,
the right side is $\phi_j$; the strict boundary gives
$\phi_d>\phi_j$.  Both cases are impossible.
\end{proof}

Thus the first possible escape from odd-cycle rounding needs an external rich
row to enlarge the overload footprint beyond a singleton.  This is exactly
what happens at the sharp $2\mid4$ co-singleton packet.  On any three chosen
cycle rows, two alternating co-singletons cover the fourth row twice, so
$F$ has two rich players.  More explicitly, at $h=4$ relabel the rich block
as $H=\{r_1,r_2,k_1,k_2\}$.  In the integral family
\eqref{eq:lx-two-poorer-family}, select the poor-free coalition $H\setminus
\{r_1\}$ and the $p_1$-carrying coalition with trace $H\setminus\{k_1\}$.
Their footprint is $F=\{r_2,k_2\}$, the omitted poor player is $p_2$, and
direct substitution gives
\[
 s_\phi(F)=\phi_{p_2}=D,
\]
where $D=(h-2)(h-3)$ is the scale parameter of that family.  Hence
\eqref{eq:lx-odd-cycle-footprint-budget} is attained at equality when $h=4$.
The three-rich obstruction fails because its footprint is a prohibitively
expensive singleton; the fourth rich player supplies the first larger
internal face capable of absorbing the same alternating-rounding excess.

The underloaded packet also gives a cost-sensitive bridge to the classical
set-covering polyhedron, rather than only a qualitative nonintegrality
statement.

\begin{theorem}[unit-gap set-cover bridge]
\label{thm:lx-unit-gap-set-cover}
Let $v$ be singleton-normalized and exact, let
$\phi=\operatorname{Lmin}(v)$, and let $H$ be a slack cumulative richer
suffix at a strict boundary with poorer side $\{a,b\}$.  Choose a core point
$z$ exact on $H$ and label the poor players so that, for
$\Delta=z-\phi$,
\[
 \Delta_a<0<\Delta_b.
\]
Suppose a lifted exact-cover packet of proper $\phi$-tight coalitions has
unit rich incidence on $H$ and gaining-player load $r_b<1$.

Let $M$ be its rich trace-incidence matrix, retaining duplicate lifted
columns, and give a column $T$ the integral cost
\[
 c_T:=|T\cap H|+\mathbf1_{\{b\in T\}}.             \tag{UG-1}
 \label{eq:lx-unit-gap-cost}
\]
Then
\[
 \min\{c^\top x:Mx\geq\mathbf1_H,\ x\geq0\}
 <|H|+1
 \leq
 \min\{c^\top y:My\geq\mathbf1_H,\ y\in\mathbb Z_+^{\mathcal T}\}.
                                                               \tag{UG-2}
 \label{eq:lx-unit-gap}
\]
In particular, $M$ is nonideal.  For every lifted exact-cover point $x$,
its cost is exactly $|H|+r_b(x)$; hence preservation of the strict unit gap
after lifting a minor point back to $H$ with the original costs is equivalent
to preservation of gaining-player load below one.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The given fractional packet has total rich incidence $|H|$, so its cost is
$|H|+r_b<|H|+1$.  Now take an integral cover $y$ and let
\[
 m:=\sum_T|T\cap H|y_T.
\]
Since every rich row is covered, $m\geq|H|$.  If $m>|H|$, integrality gives
$m\geq|H|+1$, and the claimed cost bound follows.  If $m=|H|$, every rich
row is covered exactly once.  Were its $b$-load zero, summing at $z$ the
tight-coalition inequalities selected by $y$ would give
\[
 0\leq\sum_Ty_T\Delta(T)
   =\Delta(H)+r_a(y)\Delta_a
   =-\bigl(\phi(H)-v(H)\bigr)+r_a(y)\Delta_a<0,
\]
which is impossible.  Thus its integral $b$-load is at least one and again
$c^\top y\geq|H|+1$.  Finally, any exact-cover point has total rich
incidence $|H|$, so \eqref{eq:lx-unit-gap-cost} gives the last assertion.
\end{proof}

The rich-incidence term can be separated from the poor label by a large
integer scale.  This makes exactness of a cost-preserving Lehman lift a
consequence rather than an additional hypothesis.

\begin{corollary}[scale-separated exactness]
\label{cor:lx-scale-separated-exactness}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-unit-gap-set-cover}, let $q$ be
the number of active lifted columns, retaining duplicates, and choose an
integer $\Lambda>q$.  Give each column the cost
\[
 c_T^{(\Lambda)}:=\Lambda|T\cap H|+
                    \mathbf1_{\{b\in T\}}.          \tag{UG-M1}
 \label{eq:lx-scaled-unit-gap-cost}
\]
Then the fractional optimum is below $\Lambda|H|+1$, while every integral
cover costs at least $\Lambda|H|+1$.

Suppose a set-cover minor has a positive Lehman core with column sum $r$,
its uniform point is feasible for every row of the minor, and that point is
lifted to a cover of $H$ by adjoining unit-weight contracted columns.  If
this lifted point, evaluated with the original costs
\eqref{eq:lx-scaled-unit-gap-cost}, still costs less than
$\Lambda|H|+1$, then every rich row has load exactly one and the
gaining-player load is below one.  Consequently the contracted columns have
pairwise disjoint rich traces, surviving columns avoid those traces, and no
contracted column contains either poor player.  In particular, a reduction
preserving this one scaled objective gap automatically supplies the full
exact-cover, deficit-preserving, poor-label-neutral lift.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The first claim repeats the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-unit-gap-set-cover}.
The given fractional packet costs $\Lambda|H|+r_b$.  An integral cover with
more than $|H|$ units of rich incidence costs at least
$\Lambda(|H|+1)>\Lambda|H|+1$; one with exactly $|H|$ units is a partition
and has integral $b$-load at least one.

For the lifted minor point, every rich row has load at least one.  Every
positive excess is a sum of integers from contracted columns and multiples
of $1/r$ from surviving columns, and is therefore at least $1/r$.  Hence
any overcovered row makes the total cost at least
\[
 \Lambda|H|+\frac{\Lambda}{r}.
\]
The Lehman core uses at most the original $q$ columns, so
$r\leq q<\Lambda$ and this is strictly greater than
$\Lambda|H|+1$, a contradiction.  Thus every rich row has unit load.  This
forces the unit-weight contracted traces to be pairwise disjoint and every
surviving column to avoid them.  The cost is now $\Lambda|H|$ plus the
nonnegative $b$-load, which must be below one; its contracted part is an
integer, hence zero.  Writing the total poor loads as $\rho_a,\rho_b$, the
packet transport inequality becomes
\[
 0\leq(\rho_b-1)\sigma+(\rho_a-\rho_b)\Delta_a,
 \qquad \sigma:=\phi(H)-v(H)>0.
\]
Because $\rho_b<1$ and $\Delta_a<0$, it forces
$\rho_a<\rho_b<1$.  The contracted $a$-load is also an integer part of a
load below one, so it vanishes as well.
\end{proof}

The corollary is deliberately conditional on preservation of the displayed
objective.  Classical minimally-nonideal reduction preserves nonideality,
not an arbitrarily prescribed separating cost.  The minor-compatible
covering/packing pairs and ideal unit-increasing set functions of
\citet{KiralyPap2016} broaden Lehman's framework, but do not state that this
scale-separated objective survives.  Establishing such a relative reduction,
or finding a counterexample to it, is the remaining lifting problem.

There is an exact unweighted reformulation of that problem.  Let
$\mathcal C$ be the clutter on the active columns whose member $C_h$ is the
set of columns containing rich row $h$.  For a positive integral cost vector
$c$, form the standard parallelization $\mathcal C^c$ by replacing column
$T$ with $c_T$ clones and replacing every member by all choices of one clone
from each of its elements; see \citet{Cornuejols2001}.

\begin{proposition}[parallelized weighted-gap identity]
\label{prop:lx-parallelized-relative-minor}
For every positive integral $c$,
\[
 \tau(\mathcal C^c)=\tau^c(\mathcal C),
 \qquad
 \tau_f(\mathcal C^c)=\tau_f^c(\mathcal C).       \tag{PAR}
 \label{eq:lx-parallelized-gap}
\]
Consequently the scale-separated gap makes $\mathcal C^c$ nonideal for the
ordinary all-ones objective.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
In an integral cover of $\mathcal C^c$, every original member contains a
clone class selected in its entirety: otherwise choosing one unselected clone
from each class gives an uncovered parallel member.  Partial clone classes
can therefore be discarded, and the selected full classes are precisely a
$c$-weighted cover of $\mathcal C$.  This proves the first identity.
