For the fractional identity, average any feasible point over all
permutations within every clone class.  Feasibility and objective value are
preserved, and the averaged point has one common value $x_T$ on the
$c_T$ clones of $T$.  Its parallel covering inequalities are exactly
$\sum_{T\in C_h}x_T\geq1$, and its cardinality is $c^\top x$.
\end{proof}

Parallelization does not itself solve the lifting problem.  An arbitrary MNI
minor may fail to remain below the original global threshold.  More
importantly, its core rows are parallel clutter members rather than original
rich players.  Averaging a lifted core within clone classes introduces
denominators $c_T r$, so the $1/r$ excess quantum used in
Corollary~\ref{cor:lx-scale-separated-exactness} is lost.  Thus the
unweighted identity \eqref{eq:lx-parallelized-gap} is an exact reformulation
of the objective gap, not a Lehman lift of its player-incidence geometry.
Minor inequalities describe all nonboolean facets only in special covering
classes, and generalized minor inequalities are needed even for broader
circulant families.  Thus ordinary Lehman reduction alone does not prove
the desired relative statement; the exact-game cost and label structure must
do additional work before taking the minor.

There is also a label-preserving balanced-matrix formulation.  Instead of
trying to carry the poor labels alongside a rich-side minor, make them rows
of the matrix before taking any obstruction.

\begin{theorem}[labeled odd-cycle necessity]
\label{thm:lx-labeled-odd-cycle}
In the setting of Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-unit-gap-set-cover}, let a lifted
packet have poor loads $r_a,r_b$ with $r_b<1$.  Form the zero--one matrix
$\widehat M$ whose rows are $H\cup\{a,b\}$ and whose original columns are
the full player-incidence vectors of the lifted tight coalitions.  Adjoin
two dummy columns $e_a,e_b$, supported only on their named poor rows.
Then the exact-cover polytope
\[
 \{x\geq0:\widehat Mx=\mathbf1_{H\cup\{a,b\}}\}   \tag{LO-1}
 \label{eq:lx-labeled-exact-cover}
\]
is nonintegral.  Consequently $\widehat M$ is not balanced and contains an
odd-order square submatrix with exactly two ones in every row and column.
Neither dummy column belongs to such a submatrix, so the odd cycle uses only
original lifted tight coalitions while retaining whichever poor-label rows
it meets.

If, moreover, the positive rich traces of the packet form a minimal balanced
collection, then extending the packet by dummy weights $1-r_a$ and $1-r_b$
gives a fractional vertex of \eqref{eq:lx-labeled-exact-cover}.  Its positive
original columns together with $e_a,e_b$ are linearly independent.  After
adjoining the right-hand-side column $\mathbf1_{H\cup\{a,b\}}$, the resulting
dependence is a linear circuit, with every packet and dummy coefficient
strictly positive.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The packet inequality and $r_b<1$ imply $r_a<r_b$: indeed, with
$\sigma=\phi(H)-v(H)$ and $\Delta_b=\sigma-\Delta_a$,
\[
 0\leq-\sigma+r_a\Delta_a+r_b\Delta_b
   =(r_b-1)\sigma+(r_a-r_b)\Delta_a,
\]
where the first term is negative and $\Delta_a<0$.  Thus both poor loads
are below one.  Extend the packet by assigning dummy weights $1-r_a$ and
$1-r_b$.  This gives a feasible point of
\eqref{eq:lx-labeled-exact-cover} with strictly positive $e_b$ coordinate.

For the additional assertion, let $M_S$ be the rich trace-incidence matrix
on the positive packet support.  A minimal balanced collection has unique
normalized balancing weights, so the columns of $M_S$ are linearly
independent: a nonzero kernel direction would permit two sufficiently small
opposite perturbations of the positive balancing vector.  The lifted full
columns remain independent because their restrictions to the rich rows are
the columns of $M_S$.  Adjoining $e_a,e_b$ preserves independence, since
these dummy columns vanish on every rich row.  Hence the displayed feasible
point is a vertex.  Also $r_b>0$, since $r_b=0$ would make the packet
inequality at most $-\sigma+r_a\Delta_a<0$.  Thus
$0<1-r_b<1$, and the vertex is fractional.  Finally, the unique expression
of the all-ones right-hand side uses every positive packet coefficient and
both positive dummy coefficients.  The independent support columns together
with that right-hand side therefore form a minimally dependent set, proving
the circuit claim.

In any integral point of \eqref{eq:lx-labeled-exact-cover}, the selected
original columns partition $H$ and load each poor row at most once.  If the
$e_b$ dummy were selected, no original selected coalition would contain
$b$.  Summing their tight-coalition inequalities at $z$ would then give
\[
 0\leq\Delta(H)+q_a\Delta_a=-\sigma+q_a\Delta_a<0,
\]
where $q_a\in\{0,1\}$, a contradiction.  Hence every integral point has
$e_b$ coordinate zero.  The displayed fractional point cannot lie in the
convex hull of the integral points, proving nonintegrality.

Balanced zero--one matrices have integral set-partitioning polytopes
\citep{Berge1972}; the contrapositive and the forbidden-submatrix
characterization give the odd cycle.  A dummy column has only one nonzero
entry in the entire matrix, so it cannot occur in a submatrix having two
ones in every column.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[two one-label odd-cycle obstructions]
\label{cor:lx-two-one-label-cycles}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-labeled-odd-cycle}, form
$\widehat M_a$ from the original lifted columns restricted to
$H\cup\{a\}$ and a dummy singleton column $e_a$.  Define
$\widehat M_b$ analogously on $H\cup\{b\}$.  The exact-cover polytope of
each matrix is nonintegral.  Hence each matrix contains an odd-order square
submatrix with two ones in every row and column, using only original lifted
tight coalitions.  Thus one odd-cycle obstruction lives on rows contained in
$H\cup\{a\}$ and a second lives on rows contained in $H\cup\{b\}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For $\widehat M_b$, extend the packet by dummy weight $1-r_b>0$.  In every
integral exact cover the original columns partition $H$.  Selecting $e_b$
would make their $b$-load zero, and their displacement sum would be
$-\sigma+q_a\Delta_a<0$, impossible.  Thus every integral point has dummy
coordinate zero, whereas the displayed fractional point does not.

For $\widehat M_a$, extend the packet by dummy weight $1-r_a>0$.  If its
exact-cover polytope were integral, this point would be a convex combination
of integral exact covers.  Every such cover partitions $H$ and has
$a$-load at most one.  Its integer $b$-load cannot be zero, again because
$-\sigma+q_a\Delta_a<0$.  Hence every integral point has $b$-load at least
one, while their convex combination has $b$-load $r_b<1$, a contradiction.

Both matrices are therefore nonbalanced.  Their dummy columns have only one
nonzero entry and cannot belong to the corresponding forbidden odd
submatrices.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[unconditional labeled-circuit obstruction]
\label{cor:lx-unconditional-labeled-obstruction}
Every slack cumulative richer suffix at a strict Lmin boundary with exactly
two poorer players furnishes an extreme lifted packet whose rich trace matrix
has the strict unit gap \eqref{eq:lx-unit-gap} and whose poor-row-augmented
incidence matrix contains the labeled odd cycle of
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-labeled-odd-cycle}; moreover, both one-label augmented
matrices have the odd-cycle obstructions of
Corollary~\ref{cor:lx-two-one-label-cycles}.  The packet extended by its
two dummy columns is a fractional vertex on the original player rows, and
its support together with the all-ones right-hand side is a linear circuit.
The corresponding support in each one-label augmentation is likewise a
fractional-vertex circuit.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Use the packet mixture in
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-balanced-trace-transport} and label its common exact-face
displacement by $\Delta_a<0<\Delta_b$.  Its average $b$-load is below one,
so some positive-weight extreme packet has $r_b<1$.  Apply
Theorems~\ref{thm:lx-unit-gap-set-cover} and
\ref{thm:lx-labeled-odd-cycle}, and then
Corollary~\ref{cor:lx-two-one-label-cycles}, to that packet.
Because the packet comes from an extreme point of the rich exact-cover
polytope, its traces form a minimal balanced collection.  The final assertion
of Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-labeled-odd-cycle} therefore gives, on the original
player rows, a canonical fractional labeled-cover circuit supported by that
same packet and its two dummy columns.  The analogous support columns in
each one-label augmentation are also independent; adjoining the relevant
all-ones right-hand side gives one circuit on $H\cup\{a\}$ and another on
$H\cup\{b\}$.  The latter vertex is fractional because
$0<1-r_b<1$.  The former is fractional as well: integral packet weights
would partition $H$ and give an integer $b$-load below one, hence zero,
contradicting the packet displacement inequality.
\end{proof}

The exceptional minimally-nonideal family in Lehman's theorem is incompatible
with this underload, independently of the positive Lehman equation.

\begin{theorem}[degenerate-MNI trace exclusion]
\label{thm:lx-degenerate-mni-exclusion}
In the setting of Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-unit-gap-set-cover}, an underloaded
packet cannot have the trace support of a degenerate projective plane.
Explicitly, let its rich rows be $h_0,h_1,\ldots,h_t$, $t\geq2$, and its
columns be
\[
 R_0=\{h_1,\ldots,h_t\},
 \qquad R_i=\{h_0,h_i\}\quad(1\leq i\leq t),       \tag{DP-1}
 \label{eq:lx-degenerate-mni}
\]
with their unique fractional exact-cover weights
$(t-1)/t,1/t,\ldots,1/t$.  No lifting of these columns to proper
$\phi$-tight coalitions can have gaining-player load below one and satisfy
the packet transport inequality.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $u_0,v_0\in\{0,1\}$ record whether the lift of $R_0$ contains $a,b$,
and let $u,v$ count the leaf columns $R_i$ containing $a,b$.  The poor loads
are
\[
 r_a=\frac{(t-1)u_0+u}{t},
 \qquad
 r_b=\frac{(t-1)v_0+v}{t}.
\]
The packet inequality, $\Delta_b=\sigma-\Delta_a$, $r_b<1$, and
$\Delta_a<0$ give
\[
 0\leq(r_b-1)\sigma+(r_a-r_b)\Delta_a,
\]
so necessarily $r_a<r_b$.

If $v_0=0$, then $1\leq v\leq t-1$.  The strict load inequality forces
$u_0=0$ and $u<v$, so some $i$ has $R_i$ labeled by $b$ but not by $a$.
Select the lifts of $R_0$ and $R_i$.  If $v_0=1$, underload forces $v=0$,
while $r_a<r_b=(t-1)/t$ forces $u_0=0$ and $u\leq t-2$; choose a leaf
$R_i$ not labeled by $a$.  In this case select the same two lifted columns.

In either case their rich incidence is
$\mathbf1_H+e_{h_i}$, and their poor incidence is nonzero and zero--one.
They form a safe rooted tight cover, contradicting
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-rooted-semigroup-obstruction}.
\end{proof}

The first label cardinality not covered by the elementary pair count admits a
dimension-free Lehman separation argument.  It uses no classification of
Lehman matrices.

\begin{lemma}[three-column Lehman separation]
\label{lem:lx-three-column-lehman-separation}
Let $A,B$ be a positive Lehman pair satisfying
$AB^\top=J+kI$, with $A$ $r$-regular and $B$ $s$-regular.  If $r\geq4$,
then every set $V$ of three columns of $B$ has a row $d$ such that
\[
 |\operatorname{row}_d(B)\cap V|=1.                \tag{LH-3}
 \label{eq:lx-three-column-lehman-separation}
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Suppose instead that the three-column row-load vector
$y:=B\mathbf1_V$ has entries only in $\{0,2,3\}$.  Let $x$ be the number of
rows on which $y$ equals three.  The transpose Lehman identity gives
\[
 A^\top y=(J+kI)\mathbf1_V=3\mathbf1+k\mathbf1_V.
 \tag{LH-3a}\label{eq:lx-three-column-transpose-count}
\]
Here $A^\top B=J+kI$ is the classical transpose property of a Lehman pair
\citep{CornuejolsGueninTuncel2009}.
Hence every column of $A$ outside $V$ meets exactly one of the $x$ rows of
load three and no row of load two: its scalar product with $y$ is three, and
the only positive entries of $y$ are two and three.  Counting these
incidences and using $n=rs-k$ gives
\[
 rs-k-3=n-3\leq rx,
 \qquad
 x\geq s-\frac{k+3}{r}.                            \tag{LH-3b}
 \label{eq:lx-three-column-capacity}
\]
The diagonal Lehman intersection has size $k+1\leq r$.  Since $r\geq4$,
$(k+3)/r\leq1+2/r\leq3/2$, so the integer $x$ satisfies $x\geq s-1$.

The three columns have total incidence $3s$.  Their common $x$ rows
contribute $3x$, and every remaining nonzero row contributes two under the
contrary assumption.  Thus $3(s-x)$ is even.  Also $x\leq s$, so
$s-x\in\{0,1\}$; parity forces $x=s$.  All three columns are therefore
supported on the same $s$ rows and are identical.  This contradicts
nonsingularity of $B$, which follows from
$AB^\top=J+kI$.  Hence
\eqref{eq:lx-three-column-lehman-separation} holds.
\end{proof}

The same transpose argument has a parity consequence for four columns when
the Lehman multiplier is odd.

\begin{lemma}[four-column separation at odd multiplier]
\label{lem:lx-four-column-odd-lehman-separation}
Let $A,B$ be a positive Lehman pair satisfying $AB^\top=J+kI$, with $A$
$r$-regular.  If $r\geq5$ and $k$ is odd, every set $V$ of four columns of
$B$ has a row meeting $V$ exactly once.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Suppose no such row exists and put $y=B\mathbf1_V$.  Then the entries of $y$
belong to $\{0,2,3,4\}$.  No entry can equal three.  Indeed, if $y_d=3$,
then row $d$ of $A$ has an entry in some column $c\notin V$ because its row
sum is $r\geq5$.  But
\[
 (A^\top y)_c=4,
\]
whereas the contribution three from row $d$ cannot be completed to four
using entries from $\{0,2,3,4\}$.  Thus $y$ is even entrywise.  On the other
hand, the transpose Lehman identity gives
$(A^\top y)_c=4+k$ for every $c\in V$, which is odd.  This contradiction
proves the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[positive-Lehman trace exclusion]
\label{thm:lx-positive-lehman-trace-exclusion}
Let $v$ be a singleton-normalized exact game, let
$\phi=\operatorname{Lmin}(v)$, and let $H$ be a slack cumulative richer
suffix at a strict payoff boundary with exactly two poorer players.  Suppose
a lifted extreme packet furnished by
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-balanced-trace-transport} has gaining-poor load below
one, has a square rich trace-incidence matrix $A$, and gives every positive
trace weight $1/r$.  Suppose $A$ has a zero--one Lehman dual $B$ satisfying
\[
 AB^\top=J+kI,\qquad k\geq1,                        \tag{LH}
 \label{eq:lx-lehman-pair}
\]
where $A$ is $r$-regular and $B$ is $s$-regular.  Let $U$ and $V$ be the
sets of packet columns whose lifted tight coalitions contain respectively
the losing and gaining poor players.  For a column set $Z$, put
\[
 \Pi_B(Z):=\sum_{d}\binom{|\operatorname{row}_d(B)\cap Z|}{2}.
 \tag{LH-P}\label{eq:lx-lehman-pair-count}
\]
The packet is impossible whenever
\[
 s|V|-2\Pi_B(V)>\Pi_B(U).                            \tag{LH-D}
 \label{eq:lx-lehman-density}
\]
In particular, if any two columns of $B$ occur together in at most one row,
it is enough that
\[
 |V|(s-|V|+1)>\binom{|U|}{2}.                        \tag{LH-L}
 \label{eq:lx-linear-lehman-density}
\]
The packet is also impossible, without either displayed density hypothesis,
whenever $|V|\leq2$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The packet transport inequality gives
$0\leq |U|<|V|<r$, because its poor loads are $|U|/r$ and $|V|/r$.
For each row $d$ of $B$, write
$u_d=|\operatorname{row}_d(B)\cap U|$ and
$w_d=|\operatorname{row}_d(B)\cap V|$, and let $n_1$ count the rows with
$w_d=1$.  Column regularity of $B$ gives $\sum_dw_d=s|V|$.  Since
$t\leq2\binom t2$ for every integer $t\geq2$,
\[
 n_1=s|V|-\sum_{d:w_d\geq2}w_d
 \geq s|V|-2\Pi_B(V).                               \tag{LH-1}
 \label{eq:lx-lehman-simple-rows}
\]
If every row counted by $n_1$ had $u_d\geq2$, each would contribute at least
one to $\Pi_B(U)$, contradicting
\eqref{eq:lx-lehman-density}.  Hence some row $d$ has
$w_d=1$ and $u_d\leq1$.

Use that row of $B$ as a zero--one selector of the lifted tight coalitions.
Equation~\eqref{eq:lx-lehman-pair} says that its rich incidence load is
$\mathbf1_H+k e_d$; its gaining-poor load is one and its losing-poor load is
at most one.  It is therefore a safe rooted tight cover, forbidden by
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-rooted-semigroup-obstruction}.

If $B$ is column-linear, every pair of columns contributes at most one to
\eqref{eq:lx-lehman-pair-count}.  Thus
$\Pi_B(Z)\leq\binom{|Z|}{2}$, and
\eqref{eq:lx-linear-lehman-density} implies
\eqref{eq:lx-lehman-density}.

Finally, the density condition is automatic when $|V|\leq2$.  For
$|V|=1$, $\Pi_B(V)=0$ and $|U|=0$.  For $|V|=2$, one has $|U|\leq1$ and
$\Pi_B(U)=0$.  The two columns of $B$ are distinct because
\eqref{eq:lx-lehman-pair} is nonsingular, so their codegree is at most
$s-1$.  Hence
$s|V|-2\Pi_B(V)\geq2>0$.
\end{proof}

The real-valued overload budget also permits interpolation with a dual row
that meets the gaining labels more than once.  This yields a row test strictly
at the level of the original packet, without an integer safe-cover
requirement.

\begin{corollary}[two-sided convex Lehman-row exclusion]
\label{cor:lx-convex-lehman-row}
Under the hypotheses and notation of
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-positive-lehman-trace-exclusion}, put
$p=|U|$, $\ell=|V|$, and for a row $d$ of $B$ write
\[
 u_d=|\operatorname{row}_d(B)\cap U|,\qquad
 w_d=|\operatorname{row}_d(B)\cap V|.
\]
Define its label residual by
\[
 F_d:=(r-p)w_d+p-\ell-(r-\ell)u_d.                 \tag{LH-R}
 \label{eq:lx-lehman-label-residual}
\]
The packet is impossible if some row satisfies either
\begin{align}
 w_d&\geq1,&0\leq F_d\leq k(r-\ell),               \tag{LH-C${}_+$}
 \label{eq:lx-convex-lehman-positive-band}\\
 u_d&\geq1,&-k(r-p)\leq F_d\leq0.                  \tag{LH-C${}_-$}
 \label{eq:lx-convex-lehman-negative-band}
\end{align}
The positive band is equivalently
\[
 rw_d-\ell-k(r-\ell)
 \ \leq\ p(w_d-1)+u_d(r-\ell)
 \ \leq\ rw_d-\ell.                              \tag{LH-C}
 \label{eq:lx-convex-lehman-band}
\]
For $w_d=1,u_d\leq1$, it contains the safe-row conclusion used in
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-positive-lehman-trace-exclusion}.
More strongly, writing $F=(F_d)_d$, the residuals obey the local transport
identity
\[
 A^\top F
 =k\bigl((r-p)\mathbf1_V-(r-\ell)\mathbf1_U\bigr). \tag{LH-T}
 \label{eq:lx-lehman-residual-transport}
\]
Thus every packet column $c\notin U\cup V$ has the exact trace balance
\[
 \sum_{d\in\operatorname{col}_c(A)}F_d=0.          \tag{LH-T${}_0$}
 \label{eq:lx-lehman-neutral-column-balance}
\]
If neither exclusion band applies anywhere, every such neutral column meets
both a positive-residual row and a negative-residual row.
Summing the local identity gives the conservation law
\[
 \sum_d F_d=k(\ell-p)>0.                            \tag{LH-S}
 \label{eq:lx-lehman-residual-sum}
\]
Since $w_d=0$ implies $F_d<0$, some row with $w_d\geq1$ has positive
residual.  Thus any packet not excluded by the corollary must have a row
with $F_d>k(r-\ell)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The uniform packet has losing and gaining loads
\[
 \alpha:=\frac{p}{r}<\beta:=\frac{\ell}{r}<1.
\]
For a row with $w_d\geq1$, put
\[
 t:=\frac{1-\beta}{w_d-\beta}\in(0,1].
\]
Take weight $1-t$ on the uniform packet and weight $t$ on the zero--one
coalition selector given by row $d$ of $B$.  The Lehman identity makes the
rich load $\mathbf1_H+tk e_d$.  The gaining-poor load is exactly
$(1-t)\beta+t w_d=1$, while the losing-poor load is
\[
 a_d:=(1-t)\alpha+t u_d
 =\frac{p(w_d-1)+u_d(r-\ell)}{rw_d-\ell}.           \tag{LH-C1}
 \label{eq:lx-convex-lehman-losing-load}
\]
The inequalities in \eqref{eq:lx-convex-lehman-positive-band} are exactly
$a_d\leq1$ and $tk\geq1-a_d$.

Apply Corollary~\ref{cor:lx-exact-face-overload} to this nonnegative real
combination.  It gives
\[
 tk\,\phi_d\leq(1-a_d)\phi_a.                       \tag{LH-C2}
 \label{eq:lx-convex-lehman-overload}
\]
If $a_d<1$, then $tk\geq1-a_d>0$ and the strict boundary
$\phi_d>\phi_a$ contradicts \eqref{eq:lx-convex-lehman-overload}.  If
$a_d=1$, the right side of \eqref{eq:lx-convex-lehman-overload} is zero
while $tk\phi_d>0$, again a contradiction.

When $w_d=1$, one has $t=1$.  The positive band reduces to
$(1-k)(r-\ell)\leq u_d(r-\ell)\leq r-\ell$, which holds for
$u_d\in\{0,1\}$ because $k\geq1$.

For the negative band, put
\[
 \widehat t:=\frac{1-\alpha}{u_d-\alpha}
 =\frac{r-p}{ru_d-p}\in(0,1].
\]
Interpolate with the same dual-row selector using weight $\widehat t$.
The losing-poor load is now exactly one, while the gaining-poor load is
\[
 b_d:=(1-\widehat t)\beta+\widehat t w_d
 =\frac{\ell(u_d-1)+w_d(r-p)}{ru_d-p}.              \tag{LH-C3}
 \label{eq:lx-convex-lehman-gaining-load}
\]
The inequality $F_d\leq0$ is exactly $b_d\leq1$, and
$F_d\geq-k(r-p)$ is exactly $\widehat t k\geq1-b_d$.
Corollary~\ref{cor:lx-exact-face-overload} gives
\[
 \widehat t k\phi_d\leq(1-b_d)\phi_b,             \tag{LH-C4}
 \label{eq:lx-convex-lehman-negative-overload}
\]
which contradicts $\phi_d>\phi_b$ exactly as above, including the endpoint
$b_d=1$.

For the final claims, regard $u=B\mathbf1_U$, $w=B\mathbf1_V$, and
$F=(r-p)w+(p-\ell)\mathbf1-(r-\ell)u$.  The classical transpose identity
$A^\top B=J+kI$ and $A^\top\mathbf1=r\mathbf1$ give
\begin{align*}
 A^\top F
 &=(r-p)(\ell\mathbf1+k\mathbf1_V)
   +(p-\ell)r\mathbf1
   -(r-\ell)(p\mathbf1+k\mathbf1_U)\\
 &=k\bigl((r-p)\mathbf1_V-(r-\ell)\mathbf1_U\bigr),
\end{align*}
because the common all-ones coefficient cancels.  This proves
\eqref{eq:lx-lehman-residual-transport} and its neutral-column specialization.
Left-multiply the transport identity by $\mathbf1^\top$.  Since every column
of $A$ has sum $r$, its left side is $r\sum_dF_d$, while its right side is
\[
 k\bigl((r-p)\ell-(r-\ell)p\bigr)=kr(\ell-p).
\]
This proves \eqref{eq:lx-lehman-residual-sum}.
If $w_d=0$, then
$F_d=p-\ell-(r-\ell)u_d<0$.  Conversely, a zero residual on a row with
$w_d\geq1$ lies in the positive exclusion band.  Hence a surviving packet
has no zero residuals, and
\eqref{eq:lx-lehman-neutral-column-balance} forces both signs in every
neutral column.  The zero-$V$ observation also proves the final assertion of
the corollary.
\end{proof}

The separation lemma closes the next label cardinality without a density or
codegree hypothesis.

\begin{corollary}[three-gaining-column exclusion]
\label{cor:lx-three-gaining-column-exclusion}
Under the hypotheses of
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-positive-lehman-trace-exclusion}, the packet is impossible
whenever $|V|\leq3$.  Consequently any surviving positive Lehman packet has
\[
 |V|\geq4,\qquad r\geq5.                           \tag{LH-4}
 \label{eq:lx-positive-lehman-survivor-floor}
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-positive-lehman-trace-exclusion} already handles
$|V|\leq2$.  Suppose $|V|=3$.  Then $r\geq4$, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-three-column-lehman-separation} supplies a row $d$ with
$w_d=1$.  Put $p=|U|\leq2$.  Its residual is
\[
 F_d=(r-3)(1-u_d).                                  \tag{LH-3c}
 \label{eq:lx-three-column-residual}
\]
If $u_d=0$, then $0<F_d=r-3\leq k(r-3)$; if $u_d=1$, then $F_d=0$.
In both cases the positive band
\eqref{eq:lx-convex-lehman-positive-band} applies.  If $u_d=2$, necessarily
$p=2$, and
\[
 -k(r-p)=-k(r-2)\leq-(r-3)=F_d<0,
\]
so the negative band
\eqref{eq:lx-convex-lehman-negative-band} applies instead.  These cases are
exhaustive because $u_d\leq p\leq2$.  Finally $|V|<r$ gives $r\geq5$ for any
survivor.
\end{proof}

Thus the first unresolved positive-Lehman branch is not merely beyond the
graph-like case: it requires at least four gaining labels on a trace degree of
at least five.  This strengthens the dimension-free obstruction hierarchy
behind the sharp low-dimensional theorem.  It does not eliminate the
opposite-sign co-singleton matrix $J-I$ realized by the sharp family.

The first open cardinality has a rigid local form in the thin case.  The
following proposition is a structural reduction, not an exclusion theorem.

\begin{proposition}[thin four-label normal form]
\label{prop:lx-thin-four-label-normal-form}
Under the hypotheses of
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-positive-lehman-trace-exclusion}, suppose $k=1$,
$|V|=4$, and neither residual band excludes the packet.  Then
\[
 r\geq6,\qquad |U|=3,\qquad |U\cap V|\leq1.         \tag{LH-4a}
 \label{eq:lx-four-label-parameters}
\]
Every row with $w_d=1$ has $u_d=3$, and every row with $u_d=1$ has
$w_d\geq2$.

Moreover, let $c\notin U\cup V$ be a label-free packet column.  Ignore rows
$d\in\operatorname{col}_c(A)$ with $(w_d,u_d)=(0,0)$.  The multiset of all
remaining load pairs in that trace is exactly one of
\[
\begin{array}{c|l}
 1&(4,3)\\
 2&(1,3),(3,0)\\
 3&(0,3),(4,0)\\
 4&(0,3),(2,0),(2,0)\\
 5&(0,2),(4,1)\\
 6&(0,2),(2,0),(2,1).
\end{array}                                         \tag{LH-4b}
\label{eq:lx-four-label-local-types}
\]
If $|U\cap V|=1$, only types $1,2,5,6$ are feasible.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-four-column-odd-lehman-separation} supplies a row with
$w_d=1$.  For $\ell=4$, such a row has
\[
 F_d=(r-4)(1-u_d).
\]
The positive band excludes $u_d\in\{0,1\}$, and the negative band excludes
$u_d=2$.  If $p:=|U|\leq2$, these cases are exhaustive, so $p=3$ and every
$V$-simple row has $u_d=3$.  When $r=5$, the remaining value $u_d=3$ gives
$F_d=-2=-k(r-p)$ and is also excluded.  Hence $r\geq6$.  A row containing all
three $U$-columns and only one $V$-column is impossible when
$|U\cap V|\geq2$, proving the overlap bound.

Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-three-column-lehman-separation} to the three columns
of $U$.  More generally, on any row with $u_d=1$ one has
\[
 F_d=(r-3)(w_d-1).
\]
The negative band excludes $w_d=0$ at equality, and either band excludes
$w_d=1$.  Thus every such row has $w_d\geq2$.

Now fix $c\notin U\cup V$.  The transpose Lehman identity gives
\[
 \sum_{d\in\operatorname{col}_c(A)}w_d=4,
 \qquad
 \sum_{d\in\operatorname{col}_c(A)}u_d=3.          \tag{LH-4c}
 \label{eq:lx-four-label-neutral-loads}
\]
The two residual bands leave the following nonzero row types:
\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
 w_d&\text{possible }u_d\\ \hline
 0&2,3\\
 1&3\\
 2&0,1\\
 3&0,1,2\\
 4&0,1,2,3.
\end{array}                                         \tag{LH-4d}
\label{eq:lx-four-label-row-types}
\]
Partition the total $w$-load four.  A part four yields types $1,3,5$ after
filling the remaining $u$-load; a $3+1$ partition yields type $2$; and a
$2+2$ partition yields types $4,6$.  Two or more parts of size one already
have total $u$-load at least six.  This proves that
\eqref{eq:lx-four-label-local-types} is exhaustive.  If $|U\cap V|=1$, a row
with $w_d=0$ cannot have $u_d=3$, eliminating types $3,4$.
\end{proof}

The local load restrictions already identify a canonical column globally.
This is the first point at which the six local tiles collapse to an exact
Lehman incidence rather than merely a list of possibilities.

\begin{proposition}[heavy-row column rigidity]
\label{prop:lx-four-label-heavy-column}
Under the hypotheses of
Proposition~\ref{prop:lx-thin-four-label-normal-form}, let
\[
 H_U:=\{d:u_d\geq2\}.
\]
There is a unique column $a_*\in U$ whose $A$-trace contains two rows of
$H_U$; every other $A$-column contains exactly one.  Moreover
\[
 |H_U|=s,\qquad H_U=\operatorname{col}_{a_*}(B).   \tag{LH-4e}
 \label{eq:lx-four-label-heavy-column}
\]
Equivalently, a row of $B$ contains $a_*$ if and only if it contains at
least two columns of $U$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For every column $c\notin U$, the transpose Lehman identity gives
\[
 \sum_{d\in\operatorname{col}_c(A)}u_d=3.
\]
If $c$ is label-free, the six types in
\eqref{eq:lx-four-label-local-types} show directly that its trace contains
exactly one row of $H_U$.  If $c\in V\setminus U$, three rows with $u_d=1$
would have total $w$-load at least six by
Proposition~\ref{prop:lx-thin-four-label-normal-form}, whereas the transpose
identity gives total $w$-load five.  Thus this trace also contains exactly
one heavy row.

For $c\in U$, the total $u$-load is four.  There can be at most two heavy
rows.  There must be at least one: otherwise four rows have $u_d=1$ and
therefore total $w$-load at least eight, while the actual total is four when
$c\notin V$ and five when $c\in V$.  Hence every $U$-column contains one or
two rows of $H_U$.

Let $t$ be the number of $U$-columns containing two heavy rows.  Double-count
the incidences between $H_U$ and the columns of $A$.  Every column outside
$U$ contributes one, while the three columns of $U$ contribute $3+t$.
Therefore
\[
 r|H_U|=(n-3)+(3+t)=n+t=rs-1+t.                   \tag{LH-4f}
 \label{eq:lx-four-label-heavy-count}
\]
Here $0\leq t\leq3$ and $r\geq6$.  Divisibility by $r$ forces $t=1$ and
$|H_U|=s$.  If $a_*$ is the unique doubled column, the trace counts say
\[
 A^\top\mathbf1_{H_U}=\mathbf1+\mathbf1_{\{a_*\}}.
\]
But $A^\top B=J+I$ gives the same vector as
$A^\top B\mathbf1_{\{a_*\}}$.  Since $A$ is nonsingular,
$\mathbf1_{H_U}=B\mathbf1_{\{a_*\}}$, proving the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[dual-cubic four-label exclusion]
\label{cor:lx-four-label-dual-cubic-exclusion}
Under the hypotheses of
Proposition~\ref{prop:lx-thin-four-label-normal-form}, one has $s\geq4$.
Thus no positive thin Lehman pair whose dual matrix $B$ is cubic supports a
surviving four-label packet.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Suppose $s=3$.  A row with $(w_d,u_d)=(1,3)$ exists.  Since its $B$-row has
only three entries, $U\cap V$ must be the singleton $\{g\}$ and that row is
exactly $U$.  Every row with $u_d=3$ is therefore identical to it; distinctness
of the rows of the nonsingular matrix $B$ shows that there is exactly one such
row.

Proposition~\ref{prop:lx-four-label-heavy-column} gives $|H_U|=s=3$, so the
other two heavy rows have $u_d=2$.  The allowed row types
\eqref{eq:lx-four-label-row-types} give $w_d\in\{0,3,4\}$ on a row of
$u$-load two.  Here $w_d=4$ is impossible because the row has size three.
The value $w_d=3$ is also impossible: a three-entry row contained in $V$
cannot contain two entries of $U$, because $U\cap V=\{g\}$.  Hence both rows
have $w_d=0$.  They avoid $g$ and contain the other two columns of $U$.
The unique $u_d=3$ row contains those same two columns.  Consequently the two
noncommon columns of $U$ have identical $B$-columns, namely $H_U$, contrary
to nonsingularity of $B$.
\end{proof}

The rows that carry the negative extreme in the preceding normal form cannot
overlap arbitrarily on the primal side.  The classical triple-intersection
property of a Lehman pair turns them into a genuine packing.

\begin{proposition}[simple-row star packing]
\label{prop:lx-four-label-simple-row-star}
Under the hypotheses of
Proposition~\ref{prop:lx-thin-four-label-normal-form}, put
\[
 Q:=\{d:u_d=3\}.
\]
The supports $\operatorname{row}_d(A)$, $d\in Q$, are pairwise disjoint.
Consequently
\[
 1\leq \#\{d:w_d=1\}\leq |Q|\leq s-1.             \tag{LH-4g}
 \label{eq:lx-four-label-simple-row-packing}
\]
Moreover, every row $d$ with $(w_d,u_d)=(1,3)$ contains at least $r-5$
columns $c\notin U\cup V$ in its $A$-support.  On each such column the
neutral trace is necessarily type~$2$ of
\eqref{eq:lx-four-label-local-types}: apart from zero-load rows it consists
of $d$ and a unique row $e$ with
\[
 (w_e,u_e)=(3,0).                                   \tag{LH-4h}
 \label{eq:lx-four-label-simple-row-companion}
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Fix distinct columns $a,b\in U$.  The column form of the classical
triple-intersection property, applied to the swapped Lehman pair $(B,A)$,
says
that for every column $c$ of $A$,
\[
 \bigl|\operatorname{col}_a(B)\cap
        \operatorname{col}_b(B)\cap
        \operatorname{col}_c(A)\bigr|\leq1.         \tag{LH-4i}
\label{eq:lx-four-label-triple-intersection}
\]
Indeed, if $c\ne a$, the displayed intersection is contained in
$\operatorname{col}_a(B)\cap\operatorname{col}_c(A)$, which has size one by
$B^\top A=J+I$; if $c=a$, use $b\ne c$ instead.
Every row in $Q$ belongs to both $B$-columns $a,b$.  Hence two rows in $Q$
cannot belong to the same $A$-column, which proves that their $A$-supports
are disjoint.  Each support has size $r$, while there are $n=rs-1$ columns,
so $r|Q|\leq rs-1$ and therefore $|Q|\leq s-1$.  The lower bounds follow
from Proposition~\ref{prop:lx-thin-four-label-normal-form} and
Lemma~\ref{lem:lx-four-column-odd-lehman-separation}.

Now let $(w_d,u_d)=(1,3)$ and put $L=U\cup V$.  If $U\cap V$ is empty, then
$B$-row $d$ contains four labels of $L$, leaving three labels of $L$ outside
that row.  If $|U\cap V|=1$, its unique $V$-label must be the common label,
so it contains three labels of $L$ and again leaves three outside.  In either
case the diagonal Lehman identity gives
$|\operatorname{row}_d(A)\cap\operatorname{row}_d(B)|=2$, whence
\[
 |\operatorname{row}_d(A)\cap L|\leq2+3=5.
\]
Thus at least $r-5$ columns in $\operatorname{row}_d(A)$ are label-free.

Fix one such column $c$.  Its trace has total load $(4,3)$ by
\eqref{eq:lx-four-label-neutral-loads}.  Row $d$ already contributes
$(1,3)$, so all other rows have $u$-load zero and total $w$-load three.
The surviving row types in \eqref{eq:lx-four-label-row-types} allow no
positive $u=0$ contribution of $w$-load one.  The only possible completion is
therefore one row of type $(3,0)$, together with zero-load rows.  This proves
\eqref{eq:lx-four-label-simple-row-companion}.
\end{proof}

The same interpolation works for an arbitrary set of dual rows.  Unlike a
general convex combination, their uniform average overloads a rich coalition
by a constant amount, so exactness can evaluate that entire face.

\begin{corollary}[subset Lehman residual face budget]
\label{cor:lx-subset-lehman-face-budget}
Under the hypotheses and notation of
Corollary~\ref{cor:lx-convex-lehman-row}, let $D$ be a nonempty set of rows
of $B$, identifying row indices with the corresponding rich players in
$H$.  Put $d=|D|$ and write
\[
 U_D:=\sum_{j\in D}u_j,\qquad
 W_D:=\sum_{j\in D}w_j,\qquad
 F(D):=\sum_{j\in D}F_j.
\]
If $W_D\geq d$ and $F(D)\geq0$, then
\[
 k(r-\ell)s_\phi(D)\leq F(D)\phi_a.                \tag{LH-B${}_+$}
 \label{eq:lx-subset-lehman-positive-budget}
\]
If $U_D\geq d$ and $F(D)\leq0$, then
\[
 k(r-p)s_\phi(D)\leq-F(D)\phi_b.                  \tag{LH-B${}_-$}
 \label{eq:lx-subset-lehman-negative-budget}
\]
In particular, under either average-load hypothesis, $F(D)=0$ forces $D$ to
be tight at $\phi$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Average the zero--one selectors given by the rows in $D$.  The Lehman
identity makes its rich load
\[
 \mathbf1_H+\frac{k}{d}\mathbf1_D,
\]
and its poor loads are $U_D/d$ and $W_D/d$.

First suppose $W_D\geq d$.  Interpolate this average with the uniform packet
using
\[
 t:=\frac{d(r-\ell)}{rW_D-d\ell}\in(0,1].
\]
The gaining-poor load becomes one, the rich overload on $D$ is
\[
 q=\frac{tk}{d}=\frac{k(r-\ell)}{rW_D-d\ell},
\]
and direct simplification gives losing-poor deficit
\[
 1-a_D=\frac{F(D)}{rW_D-d\ell}.
\]
Thus $F(D)\geq0$ makes both poor loads at most one.  Apply
Corollary~\ref{cor:lx-exact-face-overload} and clear the positive denominator
to obtain \eqref{eq:lx-subset-lehman-positive-budget}.

If $U_D\geq d$, interpolate instead with
\[
 \widehat t:=\frac{d(r-p)}{rU_D-dp}\in(0,1].
\]
The losing-poor load becomes one, the rich overload is
$k(r-p)/(rU_D-dp)$ on $D$, and the gaining-poor deficit is
$-F(D)/(rU_D-dp)$.  The hypothesis $F(D)\leq0$ and the same overload budget
give \eqref{eq:lx-subset-lehman-negative-budget}.  If $F(D)=0$, either right
side is zero, so $s_\phi(D)=0$.
\end{proof}

The corollary does not assert that every surviving labeled Lehman packet has
a zero-residual set satisfying an average-load hypothesis, nor that a tight
set obtained this way inherits a smaller positive Lehman packet.  Those are
the unresolved existence and recursion steps required for a dimension-free
classification.

Equation~\eqref{eq:lx-lehman-pair} and its role as the core identity for
nondegenerate minimally nonideal matrices are classical
\citep{CornuejolsGueninTuncel2009}.  The new claim here is only the
candidate two-poor-label exclusion and face-budget mechanism obtained by
combining that identity with the exact-face obstruction.  The alternating
selection on an odd trace cycle is the $r=2$ instance.  For a self-dual
projective-plane matrix, the linear
bound \eqref{eq:lx-linear-lehman-density} is the coarse global density bound
used below.  By contrast, the surviving co-singleton support $J-I$ satisfies
the opposite-sign identity $(J-I)I=J-I$, not a positive Lehman identity;
this locates the sharp family on the other side of the structural divide.

The same counting argument can retain noncore rows rather than assuming that
the Lehman fractional vertex covers them exactly.  The key MNI fact is that
rows of the blocker core are transversals of every row of the original
clutter; hence a dual selector cannot miss a noncore row and need only avoid
hitting it twice.

\begin{corollary}[noncore-aware Lehman rounding]
\label{cor:lx-noncore-lehman-rounding}
Let $A,B$ be an $n$-row positive Lehman pair satisfying
$AB^\top=J+kI$, with $A$ $r$-regular and $B$ $s$-regular.  Let $U,V$ be
column sets, define $\Pi_B$ by \eqref{eq:lx-lehman-pair-count}, and let
$\mathcal E$ be a finite family of additional zero--one row supports on the
same columns such that
\[
 |\operatorname{row}_d(B)\cap W|\geq1
 \qquad(d\in[n],\ W\in\mathcal E).                \tag{NC-1}
 \label{eq:lx-noncore-transversal}
\]
If
\[
 s|V|-2\Pi_B(V)>
 \Pi_B(U)+\sum_{W\in\mathcal E}
       \bigl(s|W|-n\bigr),                         \tag{NC-2}
 \label{eq:lx-noncore-density}
\]
then some row $d$ of $B$ meets $V$ exactly once, meets $U$ at most once,
and meets every $W\in\mathcal E$ exactly once.

Consequently, suppose these columns lift to proper $\phi$-tight coalitions
at a two-poorer exact-game boundary with rich suffix $\widetilde H$, the
$n$ core rows and the rows in $\mathcal E$ together represent every
uncontracted rich player, and any contracted columns are poor-label-free and
partition the remaining rich players, which the surviving columns avoid.
If $U,V$ are the losing- and
gaining-poor label sets, then \eqref{eq:lx-noncore-density} is impossible.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
As in \eqref{eq:lx-lehman-simple-rows}, at least
$s|V|-2\Pi_B(V)$ dual rows meet $V$ exactly once.  At most $\Pi_B(U)$ of
those rows meet $U$ at least twice.  For a fixed $W\in\mathcal E$, every
dual row meets $W$ by \eqref{eq:lx-noncore-transversal}, while each row that
meets $W$ at least twice contributes at least one to
$|\operatorname{row}_d(B)\cap W|-1$.  Column regularity and $n=rs-k$ give
\[
 \sum_d\bigl(|\operatorname{row}_d(B)\cap W|-1\bigr)
 =s|W|-n=s(|W|-r)+k.
\]
Thus at most $s|W|-n$ rows fail to meet $W$ exactly once.  (This number is
nonnegative under \eqref{eq:lx-noncore-transversal}.)  The union bound and
\eqref{eq:lx-noncore-density} give the required row $d$.

Select the lifted coalitions indexed by that row, together with every
contracted column.  The Lehman identity gives load
$\mathbf1+k e_d$ on the core rich rows, the choice of $d$ gives load one on
every additional row, and the contracted columns cover their disjoint rich
rows once.  The poor load is nonzero and zero--one.  This is a safe rooted
tight cover, forbidden by
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-rooted-semigroup-obstruction}.
\end{proof}

For an exact-cover lift of a Lehman minor, the apparent label-preservation
problem has a useful one-sided resolution.  It is enough to preserve the
gaining-player deficit; the transport inequality then removes both poor
labels from every contracted column automatically.  A general MNI minor may
also have noncore rich rows overcovered by its Lehman fractional vertex; the
corollary does not suppress that separate excess-row issue.  If instead a
minor lift preserves the scaled gap of
Corollary~\ref{cor:lx-scale-separated-exactness}, even one such excess is
already too expensive, so exact coverage and the gaining-player deficit
follow simultaneously.

\begin{corollary}[deficit-preserving Lehman-minor lift]
\label{cor:lx-deficit-preserving-lehman-minor}
Let $v$ be singleton-normalized and exact, let
$\phi=\operatorname{Lmin}(v)$, and let $H$ be a slack cumulative richer
suffix at a strict boundary with poorer side $\{a,b\}$.  Let $z$ be a core
point exact on $H$, put $\Delta=z-\phi$, and label the poor players so that
$\Delta_a<0<\Delta_b$.  Let
$\mathcal F$ be lifted proper $\phi$-tight coalitions, taken with unit
weight, whose pairwise disjoint rich traces partition $H\setminus K$ for
some nonempty $K\subseteq H$.  On the remaining rows $K$, suppose lifted
tight coalitions have rich traces contained in $K$, square trace matrix $A$,
uniform weight $1/r$, and a positive Lehman dual $B$ satisfying
\[
 AB^\top=J+kI,
\]
with $A$ $r$-regular and $B$ $s$-regular.  Let $U,V$ label the core
columns containing respectively $a,b$, and let $t_a,t_b$ count the members
of $\mathcal F$ containing respectively $a,b$.

If the combined lifted packet has gaining-player load below one,
\[
 t_b+\frac{|V|}{r}<1,                              \tag{LM-1}
 \label{eq:lx-lehman-minor-deficit}
\]
then
\[
 t_a=t_b=0,
 \qquad 0\leq |U|<|V|<r.                           \tag{LM-2}
 \label{eq:lx-lehman-minor-labels}
\]
Consequently the combined packet is impossible whenever
\eqref{eq:lx-lehman-density} holds (or under either of its sufficient
conditions in Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-positive-lehman-trace-exclusion}).

Without a density hypothesis, put $T=s|V|$.  There is a nonempty proper
$D\subset K$ with $|D|=T$ and, writing
\[
 u(D):=\sum_{d\in D}|\operatorname{row}_d(B)\cap U|,
\]
one has $u(D)<T$ and
\[
 \phi(H)-v(H)<s_\phi(D)
 \leq\frac{T-u(D)}{k}\phi_a.                       \tag{LM-3}
 \label{eq:lx-lehman-minor-face}
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Write $\sigma=\phi(H)-v(H)>0$.  Summing at the exact point $z$ all
tight-coalition inequalities in the combined packet gives
\[
 -\sigma+
 \left(t_a+\frac{|U|}{r}\right)\Delta_a+
 \left(t_b+\frac{|V|}{r}\right)\Delta_b\geq0.      \tag{LM-4}
 \label{eq:lx-lehman-minor-transport}
\]
Condition~\eqref{eq:lx-lehman-minor-deficit} first gives $t_b=0$ and
$|V|<r$.  Since $\Delta_b=\sigma-\Delta_a$, the left side of
\eqref{eq:lx-lehman-minor-transport} is
\[
 \left(\frac{|V|}{r}-1\right)\sigma+
 \left(t_a+\frac{|U|-|V|}{r}\right)\Delta_a.
\]
The first term is strictly negative and $\Delta_a<0$.  Hence the second
coefficient is negative, so $rt_a+|U|<|V|$.  This proves
\eqref{eq:lx-lehman-minor-labels}.  In particular every member of
$\mathcal F$ is poor-label-free.

Under the density condition, the counting argument in
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-positive-lehman-trace-exclusion} supplies a row $d$ of
$B$ meeting $V$ once and $U$ at most once.  Select that dual row together
with every member of $\mathcal F$.  The latter cover $H\setminus K$ once,
and the Lehman identity gives load $\mathbf1_K+k e_d$ on $K$.  The total
rich load is therefore $\mathbf1_H+k e_d$, while the poor load is nonzero
and zero--one.  This is a safe rooted tight cover, contradicting
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-rooted-semigroup-obstruction}.

For the final assertion, let $P$ be the dual rows meeting $V$.  Since
$|P|\leq s|V|=T$, extend $P$ to a row set $D$ of size $T$.  This is a
proper subset of $K$ because $1+k\leq s$, $|V|<r$, and
$T\leq s(r-1)=|K|+k-s<|K|$; moreover
$u(D)\leq s|U|<s|V|=T$.  Average those rows
and add every member of $\mathcal F$ with unit weight.  The resulting rich
load is $\mathbf1_H+(k/T)\mathbf1_D$, its $b$-load is one, and its $a$-load
is $u(D)/T<1$.  Corollary~\ref{cor:lx-exact-face-overload} gives the weak
upper bound in \eqref{eq:lx-lehman-minor-face}.

For $d\in K\setminus D$, select the $d$th dual row together with
$\mathcal F$.  Its displacement inequality is
\[
 -\sigma+k\Delta_d+u_d\Delta_a\geq0,
 \qquad u_d:=|\operatorname{row}_d(B)\cap U|,
\]
so $\Delta_d>0$.  Every member of $\mathcal F$ is now a coalition contained
in $H$ and is tight at $\phi$, hence its displacement at $z$ is
nonnegative.  Their disjoint traces partition $H\setminus K$, so
$\Delta(H\setminus K)\geq0$.  Since $K\setminus D$ is nonempty,
$\Delta(H\setminus D)>0$.  Therefore
$\Delta(D)=-\sigma-\Delta(H\setminus D)<-\sigma$, and core feasibility on
$D$ gives $s_\phi(D)>\sigma$, as claimed.
\end{proof}

Even when the density test does not produce one safe dual row, positive
Lehman duality still forces a nontrivial internal exact-face certificate.

\begin{corollary}[canonical Lehman internal face]
\label{cor:lx-lehman-internal-face}
Under the hypotheses and notation of
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-positive-lehman-trace-exclusion}, there is a nonempty
proper set $D\subset H$ such that, writing
\[
 u(D):=\sum_{d\in D}|\operatorname{row}_d(B)\cap U|,
 \]
one has $|D|=s|V|$, $u(D)<|D|$, and the strict slack amplification
\[
 \phi(H)-v(H)<s_\phi(D)
 \leq\frac{|D|-u(D)}{k}\phi_a,                      \tag{LH-F}
 \label{eq:lx-lehman-internal-face}
\]
where $a$ is the losing poor player.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Put $T=s|V|$.  The diagonal of
\eqref{eq:lx-lehman-pair} gives $1+k\leq s$, while $|V|<r$ and regularity
give
$T\leq s(r-1)=|H|+k-s<|H|$.  Let $P$ be the dual rows meeting $V$.
Since $|P|\leq\sum_d|\operatorname{row}_d(B)\cap V|=T$, extend $P$ to a
set $D$ of exactly $T$ rows.  Every row outside $D$ has zero $V$-load, so
\[
 \sum_{d\in D}|\operatorname{row}_d(B)\cap V|=s|V|=T.
\]
On the other hand,
$u(D)\leq s|U|<s|V|=T$.

For this row set, $W_D=T=|D|$ and
\[
 F(D)=(r-\ell)(T-u(D))\geq0.
\]
The positive subset budget
\eqref{eq:lx-subset-lehman-positive-budget} therefore gives the weak upper
inequality in \eqref{eq:lx-lehman-internal-face}.  Equivalently, this is the
average-dual-row construction with gaining-poor load one, losing-poor load
$u(D)/T$, and rich overload $(k/T)\mathbf1_D$.

It remains to prove the strict lower inequality.  Use the core point $z$ and
displacement $\Delta=z-\phi$ attached to the lifted packet by
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-balanced-trace-transport}; put
$\sigma=\phi(H)-v(H)>0$, so
$\Delta(H)=-\sigma$ and, after the packet's labeling,
$\Delta_a<0<\Delta_b$.  For every $d\notin D$, the $d$th dual row has
zero $V$-load.  Summing at $z$ the tight-coalition inequalities selected by
that row gives
\[
 -\sigma+k\Delta_d+u_d\Delta_a\geq0,
 \qquad
 u_d:=|\operatorname{row}_d(B)\cap U|.
\]
Hence $k\Delta_d\geq\sigma-u_d\Delta_a>0$.  Since $H\setminus D$ is
nonempty, $\Delta(H\setminus D)>0$, and therefore
\[
 \Delta(D)=-\sigma-\Delta(H\setminus D)<-\sigma.
\]
Core feasibility gives $v(D)\leq z(D)$, so
$s_\phi(D)\geq\phi(D)-z(D)=-\Delta(D)>\sigma$, as required.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[proper-hyperedge obstruction]
\label{thm:lx-rank-two-trace-exclusion}
Let $v$ be a singleton-normalized exact game, let
$\phi=\operatorname{Lmin}(v)$, and let $H$ be a cumulative richer suffix at
a strict payoff boundary with exactly two poorer players
$L=\{a,b\}$.  If $H$ is slack at $\phi$, label the poor players by the
exact-face displacement $\Delta_a<0<\Delta_b$.  Every lifted extreme packet
from Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-balanced-trace-transport} whose gaining-player load
satisfies $r_b<1$ contains a positive proper rich trace $R$ with
\[
 3\leq |R|\leq |H|-1.                                \tag{GH}
 \label{eq:lx-genuine-hypergraph-trace}
\]
Such a packet always exists.  Consequently every slack two-poorer boundary
has $|H|\geq4$; in particular, if $|H|\leq3$, then $H$ is tight.  More
generally, $H$ is tight whenever every proper active rich trace has
cardinality at most two.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Suppose $s:=\phi(H)-v(H)>0$.  Use the lifted extreme-point packet mixture in
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-balanced-trace-transport}, relabeling the poor players so
that
\[
 \Delta_a<0<\Delta_b,\qquad \Delta_a+\Delta_b=s.
 \tag{RT-1}\label{eq:lx-rank-two-signs}
\]
Fix any positive-weight lifted extreme packet with $r_b<1$ and suppose,
toward a contradiction, that it has no proper trace of cardinality at least
three.  Its minimal balanced trace support cannot contain $H$: the singleton
collection $\{H\}$ is already balanced, so minimality would force it to be
the whole support with weight one; then $r_b<1$ would give $r_b=0$, and the
packet inequality $-s+r_a\Delta_a\geq0$ would be impossible.  Hence every
positive trace of the fixed packet has size at most two.

Its support is therefore a disjoint union of singleton traces and pair
traces of weight one, together with vertex-disjoint odd cycles whose pair
traces have weight one half.  Here is the elementary argument.  The positive
incidence columns at an extreme point are linearly independent.  Consider a
connected pair-graph component on $n$ vertices, with $e$ pair columns and $q$
singleton columns.  Independence gives $e+q\leq n$.  If $q>0$ and $n>1$,
connectedness gives $e\geq n-1$, so $q=1$, $e=n-1$, and the pair graph is a
tree.  Choose a leaf distinct from the singleton vertex.  Its equation forces
its incident pair weight to one, so the equation at its neighbour forces
another positive incident column to zero; this also covers the two-vertex
endpoint, where the singleton itself is forced to zero.  Hence a positive
singleton component stands alone.  With $q=0$, one has
$e\in\{n-1,n\}$.  The same leaf argument reduces a tree component to a single
edge.  A unicyclic component cannot have an attached tree, again by the leaf
argument, so it is a cycle.  An even cycle has dependent incidence columns;
an odd cycle is nonsingular and its cover equations force weight one half.

An integral packet has integer load on $b$, and its transport inequality
forces that load to be at least one.  Thus the fixed packet is fractional.
All its coefficients are in $\{1,1/2\}$, so $r_b$ is a
nonnegative half-integer.  Its transport inequality
\[
 -s+r_a\Delta_a+r_b\Delta_b\geq0                 \tag{RT-2}
 \label{eq:lx-rank-two-packet}
\]
first gives $r_b>0$, hence $r_b=1/2$.  If $r_a>0$, then
$r_a\geq1/2$ and
\[
 -s+r_a\Delta_a+r_b\Delta_b
 \leq -s+\tfrac12(\Delta_a+\Delta_b)=-\tfrac12s<0.
\]
Thus $r_a=0$.  Consequently exactly one half-weight edge coalition on one odd
cycle contains $b$, and no supported coalition contains $a$.

Write that labeled odd cycle as
$e_0,e_1,\ldots,e_{2k}$ in cyclic order, with the unique $b$-carrying
coalition over $e_0$.  Replace the half weights on this component by unit
weights on the alternating edges
\[
 e_0,e_2,\ldots,e_{2k}.
\]
These edges cover every vertex of the labeled cycle once except their common
endpoint $d=e_0\cap e_{2k}$, which they cover twice.  Leave every other
packet component at its original exact-cover weights, including weight
$1/2$ on any other odd cycle.  Because no other supported coalition carries
a poor player, the resulting nonnegative \emph{real} combination of proper
$\phi$-tight coalitions has rich load $\mathbf1_H+e_d$ and poor load $e_b$.
Corollary~\ref{cor:lx-exact-face-overload}, with overload set $\{d\}$ and
$q=1$, gives $\phi_d=s_\phi(\{d\})\leq\phi_a$, contradicting the strict
boundary.

The use of the real-valued overload budget is essential when the packet has
additional odd-cycle components: leaving those components at half weight is
not an integer semigroup fibre.  Thus the argument proves the trace
exclusion, but only a one-cycle packet directly yields the safe rooted
\emph{integer} cover of Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-rooted-semigroup-obstruction}.

Hence every underloaded extreme packet contains a proper trace satisfying
\eqref{eq:lx-genuine-hypergraph-trace}.  Finally,
\eqref{eq:lx-transport-average} makes the mixture's average $b$-load strictly
less than one, so at least one such packet exists.  Its proper trace has size
at least three, forcing $|H|\geq4$.  The last assertion follows because a
proper active trace of size at least three has just been shown necessary.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[uniqueness of the first hypergraphic obstruction]
\label{thm:lx-four-rich-trace-classification}
Under the hypotheses of
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-rank-two-trace-exclusion}, suppose additionally that
$|H|=4$.  If $H$ is slack at $\phi$, then the active trace exact-cover
polytope has the co-singleton vertex
\[
 \beta_{H\setminus\{h\}}=\frac13\quad(h\in H),
 \qquad \beta_R=0\quad\text{otherwise}.             \tag{FO}
 \label{eq:lx-first-hypergraphic-obstruction}
\]
In particular, every co-singleton $H\setminus\{h\}$ is the trace of a proper
$\phi$-tight coalition.  Thus the first possible two-poorer obstruction is,
up to relabeling, exactly the fractional co-singleton packet realized by the
sharp six-player family.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Assume slack and use the extreme-point packet mixture from balanced-trace
transport, with
$\Delta_a<0<\Delta_b$ and $\Delta_a+\Delta_b=s$.  Choose a lifted packet with
$r_b<1$.  Writing $\delta=-\Delta_a>0$, its transport inequality becomes
\[
 s(r_b-1)+\delta(r_b-r_a)\geq0,                    \tag{FO-1}
 \label{eq:lx-first-obstruction-loads}
\]
so necessarily $r_a<r_b$.  The packet is fractional because an integral
packet has integer $b$-load at least one.

We first record the elementary four-point exact-cover classification.  At a
vertex, the positive trace-incidence columns are linearly independent, so
there are at most four.  The full trace $H$ can occur only as the integral
vertex by itself.  For proper traces, group the support by the number $t$ of
three-player traces.  The graph argument in
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-rank-two-trace-exclusion} handles $t=0$.  For
$t=1,2,3,4$, relabel the missing vertices of the triples and subtract the four
cover equations.  Positivity and column independence leave the following
complete list of fractional vertices, up to a permutation of $H=1234$:
\[
\begin{array}{c|l|l}
 &\text{positive traces}&\text{weights}\\ \hline
 A&1;\ 23,24,34&1;\ \tfrac12,\tfrac12,\tfrac12\\
 B&1,23,24,134&\tfrac12,\tfrac12,\tfrac12,\tfrac12\\
 C&12,134,234&\tfrac12,\tfrac12,\tfrac12\\
 D&12,13,14;\ 234&\tfrac13,\tfrac13,\tfrac13;\ \tfrac23\\
 E&123,124,134,234&\tfrac13,\tfrac13,\tfrac13,\tfrac13.
\end{array}                                             \tag{FO-2}
\label{eq:lx-four-trace-vertices}
\]
Indeed, the cases $t=0,1,2,3,4$ give respectively $A$;
$B$ or $D$; $C$; no positive independent solution; and $E$.
Substitution in the four cover equations verifies every displayed row and
also shows directly that no omitted coefficient can be positive.

Types $A$--$D$ contradict exactness.  To see this without checking poor
labels one by one, call a selection of supported lifted coalitions a
\emph{safe root rounding} if its rich load is
$\mathbf1_H+e_d$ for some $d\in H$, each poor load is zero or one, and at
least one poor player is loaded.  In types $A$--$C$, equation
\eqref{eq:lx-first-obstruction-loads} and half-integrality imply that exactly
one half-weight coalition carries $b$ and none carries $a$; a root rounding
containing that coalition is visible from
\[
\begin{array}{c|l}
A&\text{the singleton and the two alternating triangle edges},\\
B&\{24,134\},\ \{23,134\},\ \{1,23,24\},\\
C&\{12,234\},\ \{12,134\}.
\end{array}                                             \tag{FO-3}
\]
The displayed choices collectively contain every possible half-weight
support column.

For type $D$, multiply the poor loads by three.  The integer
$3r_b<3$ is one or two, while $3r_a<3r_b$.  If $b$ occurs on the weight
$2/3$ triple, no pair carries $b$ and at least two pairs carry no $a$; choose
one of them with $234$.  Otherwise exactly one or two of the three pairs
carry $b$, the triple carries no $a$, and one can choose a $b$-carrying pair
that carries no $a$.  Thus one of
\[
 \{234,12\},\qquad\{234,13\},\qquad\{234,14\}
 \tag{FO-4}\label{eq:lx-type-d-roundings}
\]
is a safe root rounding.

Finally, every safe root rounding displayed above is a safe rooted tight
cover in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:lx-safe-rooted-tight-cover}, with
$k=1$.  Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-rooted-semigroup-obstruction} rules it out.
Hence the underloading packet cannot have type
$A$--$D$ and must have type $E$, which is exactly
\eqref{eq:lx-first-hypergraphic-obstruction}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[projective-plane trace density barrier]
\label{thm:lx-projective-plane-trace-exclusion}
Under the two-poorer hypotheses of
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-rank-two-trace-exclusion}, suppose a lifted extreme
packet furnished by balanced-trace transport has gaining-poor load below one
and has as its positive rich traces the lines of a finite projective plane of
order $q$, each with weight $1/(q+1)$.  Let $A$ and $B$ be the sets of packet
lines whose lifted tight coalition contains respectively the losing poor
player $a$ and the gaining poor player $b$, and put
$C=A\cap B$, $D=A\setminus B$.  The packet is impossible if either
\[
 |B|(q+2-|B|)>|C||D|+\binom{|D|}{2}                 \tag{PP}
 \label{eq:lx-projective-plane-density}
\]
or
\[
 |D|<2(q+2-|B|).                                    \tag{PP-L}
 \label{eq:lx-projective-plane-local-density}
\]
In particular, every such packet with $|B|\leq6$ is impossible, regardless
of $q$.  Hence no projective-plane packet of order at most six can serve as
the required underloading witness at a slack two-poorer boundary.  At order
seven the only count not excluded by this theorem is $(|A|,|B|)=(6,7)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The transport inequality for the underloading packet, in the form
\eqref{eq:lx-first-obstruction-loads}, gives
\[
 1\leq |B|\leq q,\qquad |A|<|B|,                  \tag{PP-1}
 \label{eq:lx-projective-plane-poor-counts}
\]
because its poor loads are $|A|/(q+1)$ and $|B|/(q+1)$.  Put
$a_0=|A|$ and $b_0=|B|$, and let $n_k$ count the projective-plane points
incident with exactly $k$ lines of $B$.  Counting line--point incidences and
pairs of intersecting $B$-lines gives
\[
 \sum_k k n_k=b_0(q+1),\qquad
 \sum_k\binom{k}{2}n_k=\binom{b_0}{2}.
\]
Since $k\leq2\binom{k}{2}$ for $k\geq2$, the number of points incident with
exactly one $B$-line satisfies
\[
 n_1\geq b_0(q+1)-2\binom{b_0}{2}=b_0(q+2-b_0).
 \tag{PP-2}\label{eq:lx-projective-plane-simple-points}
\]
Suppose no point on exactly one $B$-line lies on at most one $A$-line.  At
such a simple point, at most one incident $A$-line belongs to $C$, because
every $C$-line is also a $B$-line.  If the unique incident $B$-line belongs
to $C$, map the point to one incident pair in $C\times D$; otherwise map it
to a pair of incident $D$-lines.  Different points yield different line
pairs.  Consequently
\[
 n_1\leq |C||D|+\binom{|D|}{2},                    \tag{PP-2a}
 \label{eq:lx-projective-plane-overlap-bound}
\]
contrary to \eqref{eq:lx-projective-plane-density} and
\eqref{eq:lx-projective-plane-simple-points}.

There is also a local obstruction.  Since $|A|<|B|$, choose a line
$\ell\in B\setminus A$.  At least $q+2-|B|$ points of $\ell$ are incident
with no other $B$-line.  At each one, failure of safe rounding requires at
least two incident $D$-lines.  A $D$-line meets $\ell$ at only one point, so
these lines are all distinct and
\[
 |D|\geq2(q+2-|B|),
\]
contrary to \eqref{eq:lx-projective-plane-local-density}.

Under either displayed hypothesis, therefore, some point $d$ lies on
exactly one $B$-line and at most one $A$-line.

Select the $q+1$ supported lifted coalitions whose trace lines pass through
$d$.  Every rich point other than $d$ lies on one selected line, while $d$
lies on all of them, so their rich incidence load is
\[
 \mathbf1_H+q e_d.                                  \tag{PP-3}
 \label{eq:lx-projective-plane-star}
\]
Their $b$-load is one and their $a$-load is at most one.  Thus the selected
pencil is a safe rooted tight cover with root $d$ and multiplicity $q$, in
contradiction to
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-rooted-semigroup-obstruction}.

Finally, $a_0<b_0$ and $q\geq b_0$, while
$|C||D|+\binom{|D|}{2}\leq\binom{a_0}{2}$.  For $b_0\leq6$,
$b_0(q+2-b_0)\geq2b_0>\binom{b_0-1}{2}\geq
\binom{a_0}{2}$, proving the uniform exclusion.  If $q\leq6$, then
$b_0\leq q\leq6$.  For $q=7$, the same inequalities leave only
$b_0=7,a_0=6$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[all-orders projective-plane exclusion]
\label{cor:lx-projective-plane-all-orders}
No line-incidence packet of any finite projective plane can
serve as the underloading witness at a slack two-poorer boundary.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Let the plane have order $q$.  Its self-dual line-incidence Lehman pair has
\[
 r=q+1,\qquad k=q.
\]
Write $p=|A|$ and $\ell=|B|$.  Transport gives
$0\leq p<\ell<r$, hence $1\leq\ell\leq q$.
Among any $\ell$ lines there is a point $P$ on exactly one of them.  For
example, the simple-point count
\eqref{eq:lx-projective-plane-simple-points} gives
$n_1\geq\ell(q+2-\ell)>0$.  Let $u$ count the $A$-lines through $P$; then
$w=1$ and the Lehman residual \eqref{eq:lx-lehman-label-residual} is
\[
 F=(r-\ell)(1-u).                                  \tag{PP-C}
 \label{eq:lx-projective-plane-two-sided-residual}
\]
If $u\leq1$, then
$0\leq F\leq r-\ell\leq q(r-\ell)=k(r-\ell)$, so the positive band
\eqref{eq:lx-convex-lehman-positive-band} excludes the packet.
If $u\geq2$, then
\[
 0\leq-F=(r-\ell)(u-1)
 \leq(r-\ell)(p-1)
 \leq q(r-p)=k(r-p).
\]
Here the last inequality follows from $r-\ell\leq r-p$ and
$p-1\leq q$.  The negative band
\eqref{eq:lx-convex-lehman-negative-band} excludes the packet instead.
\end{proof}

Thus no projective-plane packet of any order can serve as the required
underloading witness.  The argument is incidence-theoretic and does not
assume Desarguesian coordinates.  This still does not assert that every
higher-dimensional failure contains the four-co-singleton minor: general
positive Lehman cores need not be self-dual projective-plane designs, and
preserving the scaled objective gap through an arbitrary minimally-nonideal
reduction remains open.

\begin{theorem}[human two-poorer boundary threshold]
\label{thm:lx-small-poorer-threshold}
Let $v$ be a singleton-normalized exact game, let
$\phi=\operatorname{Lmin}(v)$, and let $H$ be a cumulative richer suffix at
a strict payoff boundary, with poorer side $L=N\setminus H$.
If $|L|=1$, or if $|L|=2$ and $|H|\leq3$, then $H$ is tight at $\phi$.
The conclusion is sharp: it fails when $|L|=2$ and $|H|=4$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If $|L|=1$, slack would contradict
\eqref{eq:lx-transport-signs}.  Now put $|L|=2$.  If $H$ were slack,
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-rank-two-trace-exclusion} would furnish an underloaded
minimal balanced packet containing a proper rich trace $R$ with
$3\leq|R|\leq|H|-1$.  Hence $|H|\geq4$, contradicting the hypothesis.
This proves the lower bound without a payoff-stratum case split or a finite
classification of three-player trace packets.

The earlier 16-query exact-rational classification remains an independent
audit rather than a proof dependency:
\begin{center}
\small\path{src/verify_n5_small_poorer_suffix.py},\\[-2pt]
\small\path{results/n5_small_poorer_suffix.json}.
\end{center}

For sharpness, take the lower envelope of the following six common-total
allocations:
\[
\begin{array}{c|rrrrrr}
 &1&2&3&4&5&6\\ \hline
g^1&0&1&3&3&1&2\\
g^2&1&0&3&2&1&3\\
g^3&1&0&3&3&0&3\\
g^4&1&0&3&3&3&0\\
g^5&4&0&0&0&3&3\\
g^6&1&1&2&2&2&2
\end{array}
\qquad
v(S):=\min_{1\leq k\leq6}g^k(S).
\]
Every row has total ten, every row is a core allocation of the lower-envelope
game, and every coalition attains its worth at a row.  Hence $v$ is exact
directly from the definition.  Put $\phi=g^6$.  The generator table gives
\[
\begin{gathered}
v(N\setminus\{2\})=9,\qquad
v(\{3,5,6\})=v(\{4,5,6\})=6,\\
v(\{1,3,4,5\})=v(\{1,3,4,6\})=7.
\end{gathered}                                      \tag{S6}
\label{eq:lx-six-small-poorer}
\]
The point $\phi$ is in the core and gives every player at least one.  At any
core point $x$ with $x_i\geq1$ for all $i$, the first constraint in
\eqref{eq:lx-six-small-poorer} and efficiency force $x_2=1$.  Write
$y=x-\mathbf1_N$ and $t=y_5+y_6$.  Then $y\geq0$, $y_2=0$, and
$\sum_i y_i=4$.  The middle two constraints give
$y_3,y_4\geq3-t$.  Adding the last two gives
$2(y_1+y_3+y_4)+t\geq6$.  Since
$y_1+y_3+y_4+t=4$, first $t\leq2$ and then
\[
4=y_1+y_3+y_4+t\geq y_1+6-t,
\]
so $y_1=0$.  Thus players 1 and 2 are pinned at the first Lmin level one.
The remaining four players have total eight, so their next common level is at
most two, attained by $\phi$.  Therefore
$\operatorname{Lmin}(v)=(1,1,2,2,2,2)$.  Finally, for
$H=\{3,4,5,6\}$, row $g^5$ gives $v(H)=6<8=\phi(H)$.

The fixed generator certificate, all 62 independently recomputed exact faces,
and an independent exact staged-Lmin replay are in
\path{src/verify_n6_two_poorer_suffix_counterexample.py} and
\path{results/n6_two_poorer_suffix_counterexample.json}.
\end{proof}

The six-player escape extends across an entire boundary-shape half-plane and
also separates suffix tightness from equality of the two lexicographic selections.

\begin{theorem}[internal-sacrifice half-plane]
\label{thm:lx-two-poorer-family}
For every $m\geq2$ and $h\geq4$, there is a singleton-normalized integral
exact game on a poorer block $P$ of size $m$ and a tied richest suffix $H$ of
size $h$ such that
\[
 \operatorname{Lmin}(v)=\operatorname{Lmax}(v)
 =D\mathbf1_P+2D\mathbf1_H,
 \qquad D=(h-2)(h-3),
\]
but
\[
 v(H)=2D(h-1)<2Dh=\operatorname{Lmin}(v)(H).
\]
Consequently the exact-partition condition at Lmin is sufficient but not
necessary for Lmin--Lmax equality, even within exact games.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put $r=h-2$, $D=r(r-1)$, write
$P=\{p_1,p_2\}\mathbin{\dot\cup}Q$ and
$H=\{a,b\}\mathbin{\dot\cup}K$, where $|Q|=m-2$ and $|K|=r$.  Define the
following common-total integral allocations:
\[
\begin{aligned}
u&=D e_{p_2}+D\mathbf1_Q+3D(e_a+e_b)
       +(2r-1)(r-1)\mathbf1_K,\\
z&=4D e_{p_1}+D\mathbf1_Q+2(r^2-1)\mathbf1_K,\\
w^k&=D e_{p_1}+D\mathbf1_Q+3D(e_a+e_b)
       +r(2r-1)\mathbf1_{K\setminus\{k\}}\quad(k\in K),\\
\phi&=D\mathbf1_P+2D\mathbf1_H,\\
q^j&=\phi+D(e_{p_1}-e_j)\quad(j\in Q),\\
v(S)&:=\min\{u(S),z(S),w^k(S)\ (k\in K),
                    \phi(S),q^j(S)\ (j\in Q)\}.
\end{aligned}                                                   \tag{TF}
\label{eq:lx-two-poorer-family}
\]
Every displayed allocation has total $D(m+2h)$.  Hence all of them
belong to the core of their lower envelope, and each coalition attains its
worth at one of them; the game is exact.  Player $p_1$ is zero at $u$;
players $p_2,a,b$ are zero at $z$; each $k\in K$ is zero at $w^k$; and each
$j\in Q$ is zero at $q^j$.  Thus the game is singleton-normalized.

Direct substitution gives
\[
\begin{gathered}
v(N\setminus\{p_2\})=D(m+2h)-D,\\
v(H\setminus\{b\})=v(H\setminus\{a\})=2D(h-1),\\
v(\{p_1,a,b\}\cup(K\setminus\{k\}))=D(2h-1)\quad(k\in K),\\
v(H)=2D(h-1).                                      \tag{TF-T}
\label{eq:lx-two-poorer-family-tight}
\end{gathered}
\]
The candidate $\phi$ gives every player at least $D$.  At any core point $x$
with this property, the first constraint and efficiency force $x_{p_2}=D$.
Write $y=x-D\mathbf1_N$, $E=y(Q)$, and $t=y(K)$.  Then
$y\geq0$, $y_{p_2}=0$, and $y(N)=hD$.  The next two constraints give
\[
 y_a+t\geq(h-1)D,\qquad y_b+t\geq(h-1)D.           \tag{TF-1}
 \label{eq:lx-two-poorer-family-first}
\]
The $r$ remaining constraints give
\[
 y_{p_1}+y_a+y_b+t-y_k\geq(h-1)D\quad(k\in K).
\]
Summing them and using $r=h-2$ and
$y_{p_1}+E+y_a+y_b+t=hD$ yields
$t+rE\leq rD$.  Combining this with
\eqref{eq:lx-two-poorer-family-first} gives
\[
hD=y_{p_1}+E+y_a+y_b+t
 \geq y_{p_1}+E+2(h-1)D-t,
\]
so $y_{p_1}+E\leq t-rD\leq-rE$.  Nonnegativity forces
$y_{p_1}=E=0$, hence $y_j=0$ for every $j\in Q$.  Thus the entire poorer block
is pinned at the first Lmin level $D$.  The richer block has fixed total
$2Dh$, so its next common level is at most $2D$, attained by $\phi$.  This
proves the Lmin formula, while the last line of
\eqref{eq:lx-two-poorer-family-tight} gives suffix slack $2D$.

For Lmax, the coalition $H\setminus\{b\}$ has $h-1$ members and worth
$2D(h-1)$, so every core point has a coordinate at least $2D$.  The point
$\phi$ attains this ceiling.  On the face where every coordinate is at most
$2D$, the two richer-coalition constraints force every coordinate of $H$ to
equal $2D$.  The remaining $m$ coordinates have fixed total $mD$, and their
next common ceiling is at least $D$, attained by $\phi$.  Hence Lmax also
equals $\phi$.

Finally, the saturated internal-sacrifice circuit is
\[
 \mathbf1_{H\setminus\{b\}}+\mathbf1_{\{b\}}
 -\mathbf1_H=0.                                      \tag{TF-C}
\]
The first coalition is tight, singleton $b$ has slack $2D$, and the negative
suffix has slack $2D$.  Thus this obstruction is funded entirely inside the
richer block, in contrast with the poorer-singleton funding in
\eqref{eq:lx-unique-richest-family-circuit}.  The exact parameter-grid audit
and generic staged replay at $(m,h)=(2,4)$ are
\path{src/verify_two_poorer_rich_family.py} and
\path{results/two_poorer_rich_family.json}.

This family also meets the necessary hypergraph obstruction in
Corollary~\ref{cor:lx-odd-trace-cycle} symbolically.  The coalitions in
\eqref{eq:lx-two-poorer-family-tight} have every co-singleton
$H\setminus\{x\}$ as an active rich trace.  Weighting all $h$ co-singletons
by $1/(h-1)$ gives a fractional exact cover.  Its support matrix is $J-I$,
which is nonsingular, so it is a fractional vertex of the full active-trace
polytope.  For any three distinct
$x_1,x_2,x_3\in H$, restricting the three corresponding trace columns to
the same three player rows gives
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}0&1&1\\1&0&1\\1&1&0\end{pmatrix},
\]
the smallest odd balanced-matrix obstruction.  Thus the sharpness family does
not merely have enough players: it realizes exactly the nonintegral trace
geometry forced by every two-poorer failure.
\end{proof}

The co-singleton packet is the unique first obstruction, but it is not a
universal minor of all larger failures.  The entire next support tier admits
a dimension-free classification.

The incidence geometry used in that classification is not itself new.
Minimal balanced collections and their unique balancing weights are
classical, and the three pair traces below are the familiar blown-up
three-cycle with weights $(1/2,1/2,1/2)$; see
\citet{LohmannBormHerings2012,LaplaceMermoudEtAl2025}.  The contribution of
the next theorem is instead the Lmin-specific bridge: underloaded transport
forces a unique labeling by the two poorer players, exact singleton faces
force the two sharp atom-size bounds, and one exact-game construction
realizes every admissible atom triple.  Its novelty as a combined theorem
remains subject to specialist review.

\begin{theorem}[complete three-column obstruction classification]
\label{thm:lx-three-trace-obstruction}
Under the hypotheses of
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-rank-two-trace-exclusion}, let an underloaded lifted
extreme packet have exactly three supported tight coalitions.  Up to
permuting them, there is a partition
\[
 H=C\mathbin{\dot\cup}A\mathbin{\dot\cup}B
\]
into nonempty atoms such that its rich traces and weights are
\[
 C\cup A,\qquad C\cup B,\qquad A\cup B,
 \qquad \beta_1=\beta_2=\beta_3=\frac12.           \tag{HT}
 \label{eq:lx-three-trace-tight}
\]
If $a$ and $b$ are respectively the losing and gaining poorer players, no
supported coalition contains $a$ and exactly one contains $b$.  Labeling the
last trace in \eqref{eq:lx-three-trace-tight} by $b$, exactness forces
$|A|,|B|\geq2$.  Consequently every such packet has $|H|\geq5$.

Conversely, for every $t\geq1$ and $u,w\geq2$, there is a
singleton-normalized exact game with two poorer players and
\[
 |C|=t,\qquad |A|=u,\qquad |B|=w,
\]
such that
\[
 \operatorname{Lmin}(v)=\operatorname{Lmax}(v)
 =(1,1,2,\ldots,2),
 \qquad v(H)=2|H|-1<2|H|,
\]
and its only proper Lmin-tight coalitions lift the three traces in
\eqref{eq:lx-three-trace-tight}, with the $A\cup B$ lift also containing
$b$.  Multiplying all worths and allocations by two makes the game integral.
Thus the support-three obstruction tier begins sharply at five rich and
seven total players and does not contain the four-column co-singleton packet.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let the positive packet weights be $\beta_1,\beta_2,\beta_3$.  Every rich
player has a nonempty membership pattern $I\subseteq\{1,2,3\}$, and its cover
equation is $\sum_{j\in I}\beta_j=1$.  A singleton pattern makes one weight
one.  On the two remaining columns, rank two would require both singleton
patterns and hence make both residual weights one; otherwise that residual
system is nonextreme.  A triple pattern makes every proper subset sum strictly less than
one, leaving rank one.  Hence a fractional rank-three vertex uses only pair
patterns.  Rank three requires all three of them, and their equations give
$\beta_1=\beta_2=\beta_3=1/2$.  Their nonempty row classes are exactly the
atoms $C,A,B$ in \eqref{eq:lx-three-trace-tight}.

Let $r_a,r_b$ be the lifted poor loads and put
$s=\phi(H)-v(H)>0$.  The packet inequality and
$\Delta_a+\Delta_b=s$ give
\[
 s(r_b-1)+\Delta_a(r_a-r_b)\geq0.
\]
Since $r_b<1$ and $\Delta_a<0$, this forces $r_a<r_b<1$.
Both are half-integers, so $r_a=0$ and $r_b=1/2$: no supported
coalition contains $a$ and exactly one contains $b$.  Label that coalition
over $A\cup B$.  If $A=\{d\}$, selecting the coalitions over $C\cup A$ and
$A\cup B$ gives rich load $\mathbf1_H+e_d$ and poor load $e_b$, a safe rooted
tight cover forbidden by
Theorem~\ref{thm:lx-rooted-semigroup-obstruction}.  Thus $|A|\geq2$;
the symmetric selection gives $|B|\geq2$.  Since $C$ is nonempty, $|H|\geq5$.

For the converse, put $h=t+u+w$ and let $P$ be the polytope of nonnegative
allocations with
total $2+2h$ satisfying
\[
\begin{aligned}
 x(C\cup A)&\geq2(t+u),\\
 x(C\cup B)&\geq2(t+w),\\
 x(\{b\}\cup A\cup B)&\geq1+2(u+w).
\end{aligned}                                                   \tag{HP}
\label{eq:lx-three-trace-polytope}
\]
Define $v(S)=\min_{x\in P}x(S)$.  If $t=1$, the point with
$x_a=x_b=x(C)=0$, $x(A)=2u+2$, and $x(B)=2w+2$ is feasible.  If $t\geq2$,
use instead $x_a=x_b=0$, $x(C)=2t$, $x(A)=2u+1$, and $x(B)=2w+1$.
Within every nonsingleton block, place its total away from any prescribed
member.  Thus every singleton has value zero.  The vector
$\phi=(1,1,2,\ldots,2)$ is feasible and attains all three displayed lower
bounds, so they are the corresponding coalition worths.  The defining coalition
constraints give $\core(v)\subseteq P$, while the lower-envelope definition
gives the reverse inclusion.  Every coalition minimum is attained on the
compact polytope, so $v$ is exact.

At a vertex, at most one coordinate in each exchangeable block $C,A,B$ is
positive.  After zero coordinates are removed, the remaining constraint
columns are among the five player types $a,b,C,A,B$; every
nonsingular subdeterminant of their total-plus-three-coalition incidence
matrix has absolute value one or two.  Thus every vertex of $P$ is
half-integral, proving the integral scaling claim.

Adding the three inequalities at any $x\in P$ and using efficiency gives
\[
 2x(H)+x_b\geq4h+1,
 \qquad 2x_a+x_b\leq3.                              \tag{HG}
\label{eq:lx-three-trace-gate}
\]
Thus no core point gives every player more than one.  On the face where all
players receive at least one, \eqref{eq:lx-three-trace-gate} forces
$x_a=x_b=1$; the richer block then has total $2h$, whose largest common
lower level is two.  This proves the Lmin formula.

The feasible point
\[
 z_a=0,\quad z_b=3,\quad z(C)=2t+1,
 \qquad z(A)=2u-1,\quad z(B)=2w-1
\]
attains equality in all three defining constraints and has $z(H)=2h-1$.
Conversely, \eqref{eq:lx-three-trace-gate} gives $x_a+x_b\leq3$ and hence
$x(H)\geq2h-1$.  Therefore the displayed suffix value and unit slack are
exact.

For Lmax, the first coalition in \eqref{eq:lx-three-trace-tight} has average
two, so every core point has a coordinate at least two.  On the face where
all coordinates are at most two, the first two constraints force every
member of $H$ to equal two.  Efficiency leaves $x_a+x_b=2$, and the third
constraint gives $x_b\geq1$.  Leximax minimization therefore equalizes the
two remaining coordinates at one, proving $\operatorname{Lmax}(v)=\phi$.

It remains to identify the active traces.  Since $\phi$ is strictly positive,
the nonnegativity constraints are inactive there.  If a proper coalition
$S$ is tight, LP duality writes
\[
 \mathbf1_S=\mu\mathbf1_N
 +\lambda_1\mathbf1_{C\cup A}
 +\lambda_2\mathbf1_{C\cup B}
 +\lambda_3\mathbf1_{\{b\}\cup A\cup B},
 \qquad \lambda_j\geq0.
\]
The $a$ coordinate makes $\mu\in\{0,1\}$.  The value one gives only $N$;
with $\mu=0$, comparing the $b,C,A,B$ coordinates of the zero--one vector
leaves exactly the three unit choices for $\lambda$.  Thus
\eqref{eq:lx-three-trace-tight} is the complete proper tight list.

Writing the three trace weights as $\beta_1,\beta_2,\beta_3$, the equations at
any member of $C$, at any member of $A$, and at any member of $B$ are
\[
 \beta_1+\beta_2=1,
 \qquad\beta_1+\beta_3=1,
 \qquad\beta_2+\beta_3=1.
\]
Hence their unique exact cover is $(1/2,1/2,1/2)$.  The saturated
exact-balanced identity is
\[
 \mathbf1_{C\cup A}+\mathbf1_{C\cup B}
 +\mathbf1_{\{b\}\cup A\cup B}+\mathbf1_{\{a\}}
 -\mathbf1_H=\mathbf1_N.                             \tag{HC}
\label{eq:lx-three-trace-circuit}
\]
The three nontrivial positive terms are tight, while singleton $a$ and the
negative suffix both have slack one.  The exact vertex audit, complete tight
list, and generic staged replay of the seven-player base case are in
\path{src/verify_three_trace_half_integral_family.py} and
\path{results/three_trace_half_integral_family.json}.
\end{proof}

There is a second external-sacrifice family.  It extends the three-poorer,
two-richer geometry of the five-player example simultaneously in both block
sizes and closes the region between the two preceding families.

\begin{theorem}[universal three-poorer escape]
\label{thm:lx-three-poorer-family}
For every $m\geq3$ and $h\geq2$, there is a singleton-normalized integral
indecomposable exact game with a strict Lmin boundary having $m$ cumulative
poorer players and a richer suffix $H$ of size $h$ such that
\[
 \operatorname{Lmin}(v)
 =\mathbf1_{\{p_1,p_2\}\cup Q}+2e_{p_3}+3\mathbf1_H,
 \qquad |Q|=m-3,
\]
and
\[
 v(H)=3h-1<3h=\operatorname{Lmin}(v)(H).
\]
Writing $\varepsilon=1/(h+1)$, the same game has
\[
 \operatorname{Lmax}(v)
 =(2-\varepsilon)e_{p_1}+\varepsilon e_{p_2}
 +(3-\varepsilon)e_{p_3}+\mathbf1_Q
 +(3-\varepsilon)\mathbf1_H.                       \tag{TH-M}
 \label{eq:lx-three-poorer-lmax}
\]
Thus the Lmin stratum sizes are $(m-1,1,h)$ and the richer suffix has unit
slack in every member of the family.  Moreover Lmin and Lmax are distinct,
with $\ell^1$ distance $4h/(h+1)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $H=\{r_1,\ldots,r_h\}$ and put $T=m+3h+1$.  Take the lower envelope of
the following common-total integral allocations (an indicator of an empty
set is zero):
\[
\begin{aligned}
 a&=2e_{p_1}+3e_{p_3}+\mathbf1_Q
       +5e_{r_1}+3\mathbf1_{H\setminus\{r_1,r_h\}},\\
 b&=2e_{p_2}+e_{p_3}+\mathbf1_Q
       +4e_{r_1}+3\mathbf1_{H\setminus\{r_1\}},\\
 c&=\mathbf1_Q+(3h+4)e_{r_h},\\
 \phi&=e_{p_1}+e_{p_2}+2e_{p_3}+\mathbf1_Q+3\mathbf1_H,\\
 q^j&=\phi+e_{p_1}-e_j\qquad(j\in Q),\\
 v(S)&:=\min\{a(S),b(S),c(S),\phi(S),q^j(S)\ (j\in Q)\}.
\end{aligned}                                                     \tag{TH}
\label{eq:lx-three-poorer-family}
\]
Every row has total $T$, so every row lies in the core of the lower-envelope
game and every coalition attains its worth at a row.  Hence $v$ is exact.
Players $p_1,p_2,p_3$ are zero respectively at $b,a,c$; every $j\in Q$ is
zero at $q^j$; the first $h-1$ rich players are zero at $c$ and $r_h$ is
zero at $a$.  Thus the game is singleton-normalized.

Direct substitution gives
\[
\begin{gathered}
 v(N\setminus\{j\})=T-1\quad(j\in Q),\\
 v(H\cup\{p_3\})=3h+2,\qquad
 v(H\cup\{p_1\})=3h+1,\qquad v(H)=3h-1.       \tag{TH-T}
 \label{eq:lx-three-poorer-family-tight}
\end{gathered}
\]
The point $\phi$ is in the core and gives every player at least one.  At any
core point $x$ with this property, the complement constraints in
